# Tests

The test harness covers unit, integration, end-to-end, fuzz, race, lint
and vulnerability checks. This document describes what exists, how to run
it, what each layer is responsible for and what is added in later phases.

## Running

| Command | What it does |
|---------|--------------|
| `make test` | Unit and integration tests |
| `make test-race` | Same under the race detector (the CI default) |
| `make cover` | Coverage profile under race with cross-package instrumentation; prints total |
| `make cover-gate` | `make cover` then the gate: core packages at least 80 % together, none below 60 % (`COVER_MIN`, `COVER_FLOOR`) |
| `make mutate` | Mutation testing with gremlins on `limits`, `router` and `netutil` (`.gremlins.yaml` sets the thresholds) |
| `make fuzz FUZZTIME=30s` | Runs every `Fuzz*` target for the given budget |
| `make lint` | `golangci-lint` with the configuration in `.golangci.yml`. The binary has to be built with a Go at least as new as `go.mod`'s directive, or it refuses to load the configuration: `GOTOOLCHAIN=go1.26.0 go install github.com/golangci/golangci-lint/v2/cmd/golangci-lint@v2.5.0` builds one |
| `make vet`, `make fmt` | `go vet`, formatting check |
| `make vet-all` | `go vet` for Linux amd64 and arm64 and macOS arm64 and amd64, so the macOS port cannot rot |
| `make vuln` | `govulncheck` |
| `make check` | fmt, vet-all, race tests, lint |
| `go test -run TestProxyBasics -v ./internal/kinds/http/` | One test with output |
| `go test -bench . -benchmem ./internal/router/ ./internal/waf/ ./internal/dns/ ./internal/netutil/` | Benchmarks: routing, a clean request through the CRS with and without statistics, DNS message parsing, PROXY header parsing |
| `go test ./internal/config -run TestExampleDumpGolden -update` | Regenerate the golden dump of the example configuration after an intended default change |

Tests need no network access beyond loopback and no root. They pick free
ports by listening on port 0 and reading the bound address back from the
server.

## Layers

### Unit tests

| Package | Tests | Focus |
|---------|-------|-------|
| `internal/config` | `TestConfigReferenceComplete` (every YAML key of the schema is mentioned in CONFIG.md), `TestMinimalDefaults`, `TestRejects` (25 rejection cases), `TestListenerKinds` (tcp and forward listener defaults and 26 rejections), `TestGRPCConfig`, `TestRouteActions`, `TestIngressConfig` (including `debounce`), `TestIncludes` (fragments appended in order with defaults, scalar sections, duplicates, unknown upstreams and multi document fragments refused, empty and relative globs, world writable fragments), `TestMultipleErrorsReported`, `TestZeroTimeoutMeansDefault`, `TestDuration`, `TestRateLimitDefaults`, `TestHostPattern`, `TestExampleConfig` | Defaults, every validation rule, error aggregation, the shipped example |
| `internal/router` | `TestMatch`, `TestNoMatch`, `TestMatchGRPC`, `TestRegexAndConditions`, `BenchmarkMatch` | Exact versus wildcard host precedence, longest prefix, segment boundaries, methods, priority; anchored patterns ranked by literal prefix and beating a plain prefix of the same length, header exact, prefix, regex, presence and absence, cookie presence, conditioned routes before plain ones, configuration order at equal conditions |
| `internal/netutil` | `TestClientIP`, `TestCleanPath`, `TestHost`, `TestProxyHeader` | Trusted proxy algorithm including malformed hops and IPv4 mapped addresses; traversal normalisation; host normalisation; PROXY protocol v1 and v2 headers (IPv4, IPv6, UNKNOWN and LOCAL, TLVs skipped, every truncation and malformed field refused, nothing consumed without a signature) |
| `internal/ingress` | `TestTranslate`, `TestGatewayAPI`, `TestWatches`, `TestControllerAndProxy` | Translation of rules, annotations, default backends, class filtering, port resolution by number and name, ready endpoints only, placeholders for empty services, TLS secrets, every warning, long names; against a fake API server: sync and change detection, certificate files `0600`, merge into a base configuration served by the proxy, a cluster change requesting a reload, stale certificate removal, name collisions refused, an unauthorised token kept as an error with the last snapshot; Gateway API: class filtering, hostnames from the route and from listeners, prefix, exact, method, header and regular expression matches, header modifier, URL rewrite, redirect, weighted backends with a zero weight excluded, listener certificates, warnings, the merged result validating; watches: streams open per collection, a burst of events becomes one debounced sync and reload, counters |
| `internal/dns` | `TestMessages`, `TestBlockList`, `TestCache`, `TestServer`, `TestResolverNoUpstream`, `TestEncryptedUpstreams` | Header and question parsing, TTL walk and adjustment, EDNS size, truncation, sinkhole answers, pointer loops and truncations refused; block list forms and hosts file loading; LRU bound, TTL ageing, expiry, resize, purge; DNS over TLS and HTTPS upstreams round robin with a pinned CA, connection reuse, close, the wrong CA refused, upstream string forms; a full server against a fake upstream: forwarding, cache hits, negative caching, block actions swapped by policy, TC retry over TCP and truncation for UDP clients, SERVFAIL on a silent upstream, FORMERR, dropped garbage and responses, NOTIMP, client ACL, rate limit drops, status and access lines; a dead upstream |
| `internal/dns` (views and local records) | `TestAViewAnswersItsOwnClientsDifferently`, `TestAViewsBlockListAppliesToItsClientsOnly`, `TestAViewTakesTheListenersSinkholeUnlessItNamesItsOwn`, `TestTheFirstMatchingViewWins`, `TestTheViewIsInTheAccessLog`, `TestTheLocalRecordTypesAreAnswered`, `TestARecordThatCannotBeEncodedIsNotAnswered` | The claim is that one name has two answers and neither client can see the other's, so the central test asks from inside and outside the view, then asks again in the opposite order -- the order is what would expose a shared cache entry. A view's block list is driven against a client it covers and one it does not; its block action is driven with and without addresses of its own, since a list from the view with the action from the listener is a policy nobody wrote. Overlapping views check that the first match wins, not the last. Every local record type is read back off the wire (the A and AAAA data, the TXT character string, the PTR name), a type the name does not hold must be NOERROR with no answers rather than a forwarded answer, and four records that cannot be encoded must fail at load rather than at answer time. Twelve deliberate weakenings were each caught |
| `internal/dns` (DNS64) | `TestTheAddressGoesWhereRFC6052Says`, `TestAnIPv4OnlyNameGetsASynthesisedAnswer`, `TestDNS64LeavesTheRealAnswersAlone`, `TestOnlyTheNamedClientsGetSynthesis`, `TestASynthesisedAddressIsScreenedAsTheIPv4ItIs`, `TestACachedAddressIsScreenedBeforeItIsEmbedded`, `TestOnlyARecordsAreReadAsAddresses`, `TestTheALookupSharesTheCache`, `TestTheTTLCanBeOverridden` | The addressing is checked against the worked example in RFC 6052 section 2.4 -- 192.0.2.33 at all six defined prefix lengths, copied from the RFC rather than from this implementation -- plus the "u" octet being zero at every length, four prefixes that must be refused, and a prefix with bits set past its length, which must be masked rather than carried into the answer. The two screening tests are the ones that matter: a private address must not be embedded, and neither must a *cached* one after a reload narrowed the policy, since `64:ff9b::a00:e` is not inside any private range and no prefix list would find it. A TXT record holding a three-character string has four bytes of rdata, exactly an address's length, so the reader must select by type and class rather than by shape. Thirteen deliberate weakenings were each caught, four after the tests were extended for them |
| `internal/dns` (lifecycle) | `TestServeAndShutdownDoNotRaceOnTheWaitGroup` | Twenty-five listeners started and stopped at once, with `Serve` on its own goroutine, then `Serve` called again after the shutdown. Under `-race` this fails on the version where `Serve` registered its goroutines outside the lock `Shutdown` takes before waiting -- an `Add` racing a `Wait` counts one thing or the other by the scheduler -- and where the DoH server was published for `Shutdown` to read unsynchronised |
| `internal/kinds/ssh` (host certificates) | `TestAHostCertificateFromATrustedAuthorityIsAccepted`, `TestAHostCertificateIsRefusedWhenItDoesNotHold`, `TestARevokedHostKeyOrAuthorityIsRefused`, `TestARevocationBeatsATrustedPlainKey`, `TestAnAuthorityIsTrustedOnlyForTheHostsItsLineNames`, `TestAKnownHostsFileWithNoTrustedKeysIsRefusedAtBind` | A target presenting a real host certificate, signed in the test by a CA the `known_hosts` file trusts with an `@cert-authority` line, and its own key nowhere in the file: the session runs. Then five ways the certificate does not hold -- another authority signed it, it has expired, it is not valid yet, it names another host, it is a user certificate offered as a host key -- each of which must refuse the session. Revocation is checked from both sides (the host key and the authority), against a file that trusts the authority at the same time, and against a file that lists the plain key as trusted: the revocation wins in every case. An authority is trusted only for the hosts its line names, which is the check a test had to be written for because every other case dialled the host the line named. And a file holding nothing but a revocation fails at bind |
| `internal/waf` (rule agreement) | `TestARulesAgreementIsMeasuredNotAsserted`, `TestAgreementArithmetic`, `TestProposalsLeadWithTheRuleNothingAgreedWith` | The measurement against real CRS behaviour: an SQL injection that trips one attack rule at paranoia level 1 reports that rule as having matched alone, the same request with a script tag beside it reports both rules as corroborated, and one of each makes the share a half. The scoring rules are excluded on both sides, so the blocking evaluation rule is never counted as a lone attack rule. The arithmetic covers the shapes a counter can be in, including more alone than matches -- a reset between the two reads, which must not become a negative share. The proposals test is built so that hit count and agreement disagree about which proposal matters most, because a test where they agree proves nothing about the ordering |
| `internal/intel`, `internal/kinds/http` (threat intel), `internal/config` | `TestAListIsReadAsAFeedIsWritten`, `TestTheFirstListThatMatchesDecides`, `TestAListThatCannotBeReadIsRefused`, `TestOnlyAChangedFileIsReRead`, `TestANilSetMatchesNothing`, `TestTheRefreshLoopReReadsAChangedFile`, `TestAnImportedListDecidesWhatItsActionSays`, `TestAListThatCannotBeReadFailsTheLoad`, `TestThreatIntelIsCheckedAtLoad`, `TestThreatIntelThatLoads`, `TestAChallengeSectionCountsWithoutRateLimits` | The forms a feed is actually written in -- `#`, `;` and `//` comment lines, a trailing comment after whitespace or a tab, a bare address, a network with host bits set, IPv6 -- and the matching that follows: an IPv4-mapped IPv6 address is the same client, a fingerprint matches its own list and nothing else, and the first list in order decides. A file that cannot be read, or an entry that cannot be parsed, is a load error naming the line: an imported list that silently matches nothing is worse than none. A feed that changes is re-read, and only the changed one; a feed that disappears or stops parsing keeps the entries already loaded and reports the error, since a file being rewritten in place must not empty the policy. End to end through a listener: a `log` list serves and counts, a `block` list answers 403 and reaches the ban list so a trigger can escalate, an exempt route is not matched at all, and the status names the list with its entries, hits and whether the files are watched. The config tests cover every way a list can say something this proxy cannot act on, the advice about blocking on somebody else's feed, and a `challenge` list with no challenge section -- which also caught a validator that only noticed the challenge section while checking rate limits |
| `internal/intel` (feeds and indicators), `internal/kinds/forward`, `internal/kinds/dns`, `internal/filters/uploadguard`, `internal/config`, `internal/sandbox` | `TestADomainEntryCoversTheNamesUnderIt`, `TestAURLEntryMatchesAtAPathBoundary`, `TestAHashEntryMatchesAnyOfTheThreeDigests`, `TestTheWalksCannotBeEvadedByPadding`, `TestAnEntryTooDeepToWalkIsRefused`, `TestTheWalkIsAsDeepAsTheFeed`, `TestAListOnlyAnswersItsOwnKind`, `TestAForwardDestinationIsAskedAbout`, `TestAListedForwardClientIsRefused`, `TestAnImportedListDecidesAQueryName`, `TestAnUploadIsAskedAboutByItsDigest`, `TestAHashMatchAtAFilterIsCounted`, `TestAFeedSourceIsCheckedAtLoad`, `TestAFeedSourceLoads`, `TestAFeedIsAdvisedAbout`, `TestAHashListWithAnUploadGuardIsNotAdvisedAbout`, `TestDeriveFeedPaths` | The three indicator kinds that are about what a client asked for, and the matching rule each one has. A domain entry covers the names under it and a URL entry stops at a path boundary, so `/dl` is not `/download`; both walks run shortest-suffix-first and are bounded by the deepest entry the list holds, which is what the padding test is for -- walking down from the request would let a client escape a listed name by adding 65 labels, or a listed path by adding 65 segments, and a query cannot be used for it either. A digest is recognised by its length, so a feed that writes the algorithm first still parses, and a list answers only its own kind. Then where each is asked: the forward proxy's destination (a name and, on the plain path, the whole target, with an address destination never offered to a domain list and an address feed never applied to the destination), the resolver's query name (after the operator's own block list and the policy zones, with a `passthru` rule exempting a name from the lists too), and the upload guard's files, digested in the pass it was already making and counted through `Verdict.ThreatList` so a match at a filter lands in the same counters as one in the request path. The configuration tests cover every way a feed can be wrong at load -- two sources, a format that contradicts its source, a URL with a fragment, `insecure` off the loopback -- and the advice for what loads but should be reconsidered, including a hash list with no `upload_guard` to ask it. The sandbox test pins a feed's `ca_file` to a read rule and the URL, collection id and token reference to no rule at all |
| `internal/mfa` (push), `internal/kinds/ssh` (hardware keys and push), `internal/config` | `TestAPushIsApprovedOrItIsNot`, `TestTheRequestSaysWhoIsSigningInAndWhere`, `TestPushFatigueIsRefusedWithoutSendingAnything`, `TestPushCountersSeparateADenialFromAnOutage`, `TestAPushServiceIsCheckedWhenItIsBuilt`, `TestANilPusherRefuses`, `TestTheHardwareKeyDecision`, `TestWhatCountsAsHardwareBacked`, `TestOnlyAKeyInATokenAuthenticates`, `TestASignatureWithoutTheTouchIsRefused`, `TestATokenIsAcceptedWithoutTheRequirement`, `TestAHardwareKeyListenerRefusesWhatCouldNotWork`, `TestTheSecondFactorCanBeApprovedOnADevice`, `TestAPushThatIsNotApprovedIsNotASession`, `TestAnEnrolledUserTypesACodeAndTheRestArePushed`, `TestTheHardwareKeyRequirementIsCheckedAtLoad`, `TestTheHardwareKeyRequirementAdvises` | The two factors that are not typed. For FIDO2, the token's half of the protocol is implemented in the test rather than mocked -- the sk-ssh-ed25519 public key with its application string, and a signer that builds the envelope a token signs: application digest, presence flag, counter, message digest -- so a real handshake runs against the same verification code a real token's signature meets. A key in a file is refused where a token is required, a signature with the presence flag clear is refused whether or not the listener requires a token, a certificate over a file key is still a file key, and a certificate asking for the touch to be waived is refused unless the listener said otherwise. Two arrangements fail the load: a requirement with no `sk-` key and no authority to issue one, and an `authorized_keys` line carrying `no-touch-required` where presence is required. For the push factor, every way of not getting an approval is a refusal -- denied, a non-200, a `result` this cannot read, an answer whose nonce is somebody else's, and nobody answering at all -- and each case asserts that exactly one notification was sent. The fatigue bounds are tested for what they do *not* do: a second request while one waits and a third within the window are refused **without sending anything**, which is the point, since the notifications are the attack. End to end through the bastion: the number the client is shown is the number the service was sent, an enrolled user types a code while everybody else is pushed, and a denial and an outage land in different counters |
| `internal/kinds/http` (ranges), `internal/config` | `TestARangeSetIsReadAsTheBytesItAsksFor`, `TestAHundredRangesIsOneDecision`, `TestWithoutCoalescingTheSetIsCountedAsItArrived`, `TestTheRangePolicyDecidesWhatTheBackendIsAsked`, `TestRangePolicyIsCheckedAtLoad` | Twenty-two spellings of a range set against what it means: one already canonical is relayed byte for byte, overlapping, adjacent, contained and out-of-order sets become the fewest ranges covering the same bytes, a closed range inside an open one disappears into it and one running into it moves its start, two open ranges become the earliest and two suffix ranges the largest. Then what is refused: a set past the bound is dropped or answered 416 by the route's own action, a value that is not a range set at all is dropped (no dash, descending, a zero suffix, not a number, empty), a unit other than bytes is left for the origin to ignore, and a header with more specs than `maxRangeSpecs` is refused unparsed -- the reading is the work the bound exists to avoid. Two hundred ranges in one header is the amplification the feature is for, and it is checked under both actions. End to end: a route with no section relays the silliest set unchanged, a bounded route sends `bytes=0-199` where the client sent two ranges, the backend sees no `Range` at all when the set could not be brought under the bound, and a strict route answers 416 with `Accept-Ranges: bytes` and still serves a single range. The config tests cover an unknown action, an absurd bound, and the advice that turning coalescing off counts a set as it arrived |
| `internal/filters/apiabuse`, `internal/kinds/http` (api abuse), `internal/config` | `TestManyObjectsOnOneEndpointIsEnumeration`, `TestConsecutiveIdentifiersAreASequentialScan`, `TestRefusedLookupsAreTheProbingSignal`, `TestCallersAndEndpointsAreCountedApart`, `TestAnIdentityIsOneCallerFromAnyAddress`, `TestTheWindowTurnsOver`, `TestTheActionIsWhatTheOperatorAskedFor`, `TestOnlyTheConfiguredPathsAreWatched`, `TestTheSubjectTableIsBounded`, `TestAPathWithNoObjectCountsNoObject`, `TestRereadingOneObjectIsNotAWalk`, `TestTheObjectSetIsBoundedAndTheCountStaysRight`, `TestAnAbsurdIdentifierIsNotCounted`, `TestTheIdentifierIsReadWithinBothShapes`, `TestOptionsAreCheckedAtLoad`, `TestALiveFilterIsReportedAndAClosedOneIsNot`, `TestFiltersAreReportedByName`, `TestAnObjectWalkIsRefusedAndCounted`, `TestAChallengeWithNoChallengerRefuses`, `TestAPIAbuseIsABanReason`, `TestUnknownAbuseReasonIsRefused`, `TestBanReasonReferenceComplete`, `TestEveryExampleIsInTheIndex` | Each signal is driven to its edge and one step past it: objects up to `max_objects` and one more, a walk of consecutive identifiers against the same number scattered over a wide span, and a refused share against the same number of requests answered 200. The distinction the filter exists for is its own test: forty reads of *one* identifier are one object and must not be a walk of forty, which is what the first version of the filter got wrong. Callers and endpoints are counted apart in both directions (two callers on one endpoint, one caller on two), and an identity established by the chain is one caller from two addresses -- the property that makes the filter worth putting after the identity filters. The bounds are checked for what they do to the *count*, not just to the memory: the object set past 1024 keeps counting as a lower bound with `overflowed` saying so, the subject table drops the oldest and counts the drops, and an identifier of two hundred characters is not counted at all. A path with no identifier in it has no object, and a path outside `paths` is not read. End to end through a listener: a walk is answered 403 on the request that crosses the bound, another endpoint is still served, and every counter the operator watches it with is emitted with the filter's name on it. An action of `challenge` with no `challenge` section is driven too, because "challenge" must not read as "served" when it is the refusal. The registry tests are what makes those counters honest -- a filter joins it when built and leaves it when its generation is torn down, so a reload cannot leave a filter being reported that no request reaches. The config tests cover every way the options can say nothing (every signal off), the ban trigger that names `api_abuse`, and two documents that had fallen behind: the reason list in CONFIG.md and the example index |
| `internal/scim`, `internal/kinds/http` (provisioning), `internal/config` | `TestAJoinerIsProvisionedAndALeaverIsNot`, `TestADeleteDeprovisionsAndForgets`, `TestSecretsAreWithheldUnlessConfigured`, `TestWithoutTheTokenNothingIsAnswered`, `TestASecondCreateOfTheSameNameConflicts`, `TestTheListFilterIsReadOrRefused`, `TestAnImmutableAttributeIsRefused`, `TestReactivationMintsNewCredentials`, `TestTheStateFileIsTheRecord`, `TestDiscoveryDescribesWhatIsImplemented`, `TestBadRequestsAreRefusedByName`, `TestAnOversizeBodyIsRefused`, `TestTheLocationComesFromTheConfiguration`, `TestCoversIsTheBaseAndWhatIsUnderIt`, `TestTheEndpointIsCheckedWhenItIsBuilt`, `TestAnUnreadableStateFileIsAnError`, `TestAKeyIDIsDerivedFromTheName`, `TestTheProvisioningEndpointAnswersBeforeRouting`, `TestAClientOutsideTheListIsNotRoutedOn`, `TestAWrongTokenIsRefusedAndObserved`, `TestTheHostSelectorDecides`, `TestTheEndpointFailsTheLoadWhenItCannotServe`, `TestSCIMDefaults`, `TestSCIMIsCheckedAtLoad`, `TestSCIMAdvice`, `TestSCIMCredentialFilesNeedNotExistYet` | The tests run against real files rather than a fake store, because the claim is about what is in them: a create writes an enrolment the `mfa` store reads back and a key the `api_key` file holds, and a deactivation leaves the enrolment gone and the key **revoked** rather than deleted -- the record of what it reached and when it stopped. The deactivated resource is then read again, to check that it reports what is currently in place rather than what was provisioned once. Reactivation is driven too: it must mint a key that is not the revoked one, and leave exactly one active. Authentication is checked three ways -- no token, a wrong token and a token that is a prefix of the right one -- and the store is never reached without it. Fourteen malformed requests each get the status and the `scimType` RFC 7644 gives them, including the two that matter most: a filter on another attribute is refused rather than answered with the whole list (a provider that ignored it would deprovision the first user instead of the one it meant), and a rename is refused rather than leaving the credentials keyed on the old name. End to end through a listener: a provisioning request is answered before routing with the backend never reached, a client the selectors refuse gets 404 and is *not* routed on, a bad token feeds the ban list, and a token file under sixteen characters fails the load rather than the first request. The config tests cover every lock that can be missing and the advice for each one, plus the two credential files that legitimately do not exist yet |
| `internal/dns` (policy zones), `internal/kinds/dns`, `internal/config` | `TestEachRecordFormIsTheActionItStandsFor`, `TestAnExceptionBeatsTheWildcardAboveIt`, `TestTheFirstZoneDecides`, `TestAnOverrideReplacesTheZonesOwnAction`, `TestAnUnsupportedTriggerFailsTheLoad`, `TestABrokenZoneIsRefused`, `TestCommentsAreStrippedAndQuotedTextIsNot`, `TestAChangedZoneIsReReadAndABrokenOneKeepsTheOldRules`, `TestTheRefreshLoopReadsAChangedZone`, `TestTheBoundsAndTheNilSet`, `TestARelativeOwnerTakesTheOriginInForce`, `TestTheZoneNameComesOffAndAnOriginIsApplied`, `TestAPolicyZoneDecidesWhatTheRuleSays`, `TestABrokenZoneFailsTheListener`, `TestRPZDefaults`, `TestRPZIsCheckedAtLoad`, `TestRPZAdvice`, `TestDNSRPZIsABanReason` | Every record form a feed writes, against the action it stands for: `CNAME .`, `CNAME *.`, `rpz-passthru.`, `rpz-drop.`, `rpz-tcp-only.`, an address, a TXT string and a CNAME to a real name. The matching is the part that decides whether a feed does what its author meant, so it is driven as a zone lookup: the exact name first, then a wildcard on each parent, an exception for one host that does *not* extend below it, and a wildcard that still denies its siblings. Zones are ordered, and a local exception zone in front of a subscription wins. Twelve malformed zones are each refused by name rather than skipped -- a record continued over lines, a class this resolver does not serve, an A record holding an IPv6 address, a wildcard that is not the first label -- and so is a zone whose trigger is not implemented, unless `ignore_unsupported` says otherwise, which then counts what it skipped. A file rewritten is re-read, an unchanged one is not, and a file that breaks or disappears keeps the rules already in force and counts the failure. End to end through a listener: each action answered over the wire, the exception resolving through the upstream, a raw AAAA query against a rule holding an A record answered NODATA rather than with the wrong type, the drop counted as a drop rather than a refusal, and the per-zone counters in the status |
| `internal/kinds/http` (documented patterns) | `TestAVersionIsRoutedByTheMatchersThatExist`, `TestAPayloadInsideAnEncodingIsWhatTheRuleDecodes`, `TestTheXMLTargetsAreTheTwoTheEngineFills` | Two claims docs/CONFIG.md makes instead of adding a key, each driven as written. API versioning: the four spellings the reference shows -- in the path, in a media type (`Accept: application/vnd.example.v2+json`), in a header, and by expression on a query parameter -- against a default route on the same path, with each conditioned route's *other* value falling through to that default, which is the half that would break silently. Content decoding: the rule the reference prints, with a SQL injection hidden in base64 inside a form parameter, refused by `t:urlDecodeUni,t:base64Decode` and an ordinary base64 body served -- so the refusal is the decoded payload rather than the encoding. Removing the transformation, or a route's condition, fails them. The XML targets are driven the same way and for the same reason: a rule over `XML://@*` fires on an attribute, one over `XML:/*` fires on character data, and one over `XML:/invoice/total` must *not* fire, because the engine parses that selector and then evaluates it against nothing -- which is stated in CONFIG.md and would otherwise be a rule an operator believes is running |
| `internal/dns` (upstream resumption), `internal/kinds/dns` | `TestAnEncryptedUpstreamReconnectResumes`, `TestDoQUpstream`, `TestUpstreamResumptionIsWired` | The first drives three DoT queries with the idle connection dropped between them, which is what an upstream idle timeout does, and asserts that the redials resumed; then the same with resumption off, where the counter must not move at all, and then on again, because it is a setting rather than a rebuild. The DoQ test drops the live QUIC connection and asserts the redial resumed -- a QUIC handshake is the expensive half of that transport. The listener test reads `upstream_resumption` back out of the status for the default, for true and for false, so the configuration reaching the resolver is checked rather than assumed |
| `internal/sshcmd` | `TestTheLineIsSplitAsAShellSplitsIt`, `TestALineThatIsNotOneCommandIsRefused`, `TestSingleQuotesHoldASubstitutionLiterally`, `TestAGlobIsMarkedNotResolved`, `TestTheFamilyIsFoundWhateverTheCommandIsCalled`, `TestAWrapperIsNotReadAsTheFamilyItCarries`, `TestAssignmentsAreReportedSeparately`, `TestSCPDirectionAndFlags`, `TestAnUnreadableInvocationIsRefused`, `TestRsyncServerInvocations`, `TestTheRsyncCompatibilityStringIsNotReadAsFlags`, `TestGitDirectionIsTheFileMovementNotTheVerb`, `TestSFTPServerOptions`, `TestAnErrorSaysWhatItCouldNotRead` | The splitting is checked against what a POSIX shell does, because words that differ from the shell's are words the policy checks and the target does not run: quoting of every kind, a backslash inside double quotes escaping only four characters, a quoted operator arriving as data, a single-quoted `$(id)` staying literal. Then everything that is not one simple command -- operators, substitutions inside and outside double quotes, unbalanced quotes, a trailing backslash, control characters quoted or not, the length and word bounds -- each with the reason it was refused for. Then the families against their own documented invocations: scp's direction from `-t`/`-f` including bundled and repeated flags and `--`, a flag no scp server takes, rsync's `--server` and `--sender` and the fact that the letters after `-e` in a short bundle are its compatibility string and not options (reading them as flags would refuse every real client, while `-M` before it is `--remote-option` and refused), git's three transport verbs mapped to the file movement rather than to their own names, and the sftp server's options with `-d`, `-p` and `-P` refused because a start directory or a request filter the gateway cannot see makes a path rule mean something else. A wrapper (`env scp`, `sudo rsync`, `sh -c scp`) is deliberately *not* read through: it is `other`, so no rule covers it and the blanket check refuses it |
| `internal/kinds/ssh` (command rules) | `TestTheLinesAPatternWouldHaveAdmitted`, `TestAFamilyWithNoRuleIsRefused`, `TestRsyncRules`, `TestGitDirectionsAreTheFileMovement`, `TestAGlobIsOnlyAdmittedInsideASubtree`, `TestARelativePathIsRefusedWithNoPathListAtAll`, `TestAnSFTPServerRuleAsksForInspection`, `TestTheRulesAreRefusedRatherThanGuessed`, `TestARuleDecidesTheSessionNotAPattern`, `TestAnUnreadableTransferLineIsRefusedNotPatterned`, `TestAnApprovedSFTPServerExecIsInspected`, `TestAPrincipalInheritsTheListenersRules` | The first test is the point of the feature: the rule an operator means to write (`scp`, uploads, into `/srv/incoming`) against every line a pattern for it would have admitted -- the other direction, bundled flags, a path in the command word, two spaces, a path that resolves into `/etc`, an assignment in front -- each refused under the property that was wrong with it. Then rsync's directions and delete options, git's verbs read as file movement, a glob admitted only inside a subtree an allow pattern covers and never against a deny list, a path above its own root refused even by a rule with no path list, and a rule set the loader refuses rather than guessing at. End to end through a real listener: what the rule allows reaches the target although the blanket transfer refusal would have stopped it, every refused spelling is counted under its own label, an ordinary command is still the patterns' business, an unreadable transfer line is refused even where `allow_shell_syntax` is on while a non-transfer line keeps that setting's behaviour, an approved exec of the sftp server binary is held to the sftp policy's read-only and path rules with the refusals counted as sftp's, and a principal with a policy of its own inherits the listener's rules |
| `internal/config` (command rules) | `TestCommandRulesAreCheckedAtLoad`, `TestCommandRulesThatLoad`, `TestAPrincipalsCommandRulesAreChecked` | Every way a rule can say something this proxy cannot act on: an unknown family or direction, a direction twice, two rules for one family, no direction at all (which would refuse the family it names), `recursive` on a family without `-r`, `delete` on one with nothing to delete, an `sftp_server` rule without `enforce_sftp_policy` or without an sftp section to enforce, `enforce_sftp_policy` on a family that speaks its own protocol, a relative path pattern, and rules on a listener where `exec` is not allowed. Then the rules an operator would actually write, the advice that an empty path list reaches every path on the target, and a principal's own rules checked the same way |
| `internal/dns` (aggressive NSEC) | `TestOneProofAnswersEverySiblingInTheGap`, `TestAProofIsNotStretchedPastWhatItProves`, `TestTheWrappingGapCoversTheEndOfTheZone`, `TestTheGapIsComparedInCanonicalOrder`, `TestOnlyAValidatedDenialIsLearned`, `TestOnlyNSECRecordsBecomeGaps`, `TestTheDenialStoreIsBounded`, `TestTheProofLifetimeIsCapped`, `TestOffMeansNothingIsRememberedOrAnswered`, `TestJunkTopLevelNamesShareOneProof`, `TestTheNextNameMustBeReadable`, `TestASiblingIsAnsweredWithoutAskingAgain`, `TestAPurgeDropsTheHeldProofs` | What one validated NSEC gap may and may not answer. It answers every *sibling* inside the gap, including the wrapping gap at the end of a zone and names that differ only in case, and it must refuse six things: the gap's own owner and next name (the proof says those exist), a name outside it, a name under another parent, a child of the covered name, and the parent itself -- each with the reason in the failure message. Only a denial the validator called secure is learned, only from NSEC records of class IN, never with a zero TTL, and never past `cache.max_ttl`. The last test drives the real signed hierarchy through the server: a sibling costs no upstream query, an insecure zone's NXDOMAIN is not learned at all, and a client that set DO still gets the upstream lookup because a synthesised answer carries no signatures. Fifteen deliberate weakenings were each caught, two after the tests were extended for them |
| `internal/filters/wasm` | `TestGuest`, `TestBodies`, `TestLoadErrors` | A guest assembled by hand in the test (`module_test.go`): request headers set from the guest and from `config`, response header removed, access log attribute, deny from the request phase with status, reason and detail, deny from the response phase, an infinite loop hits the timeout and fails closed while the filter keeps serving with a fresh instance, `on_error: allow`, a module without the response export; load errors for a missing or relative module, ABI version 2, bad options, garbage bytes; bodies: read and echo within the limit with the state header, a body over the limit untouched and reported, request and response replacement with lengths fixed and `Content-Encoding` dropped, body access disabled, the limit bound |
| `internal/xmlsafe`, `internal/filters/xmlguard` | `TestTheAttacksAreRefusedByName`, `TestOrdinaryDocumentsPass`, `TestEveryBoundIsEnforced`, `TestDeepNestingIsRefusedWithoutRecursing`, `TestCDATAAndCommentsCanBeRefused`, `TestTheShapePolicyDecidesRootAndElements`, `TestADoctypeIsNamedAsOne`, `TestARefusalSaysWhereAndWhy`; `TestTheXMLAttacksAreRefused`, `TestAnOrdinaryDocumentPassesByteForByte`, `TestOnlyXMLBodiesAreRead`, `TestTheDocumentShapeCanBeRequired`, `TestTheBoundsRefuseAnExpensiveDocument`, `TestAnOversizeBodyIsNotAWayPast`, `TestReportModeOnlySays`, `TestTheOptionsAreChecked`, `TestTheStatusViewCounts` | Nineteen documents that are attacks -- an external entity, a parameter entity, the billion laughs, an external DTD, a lowercase doctype, an undeclared entity in text and in an attribute, a notation declaration, a processing instruction, two roots, a mismatched end tag, a duplicate attribute -- each asserted to be refused *by the rule that names it*, because the name is what an operator acts on. Nine ordinary documents (a SOAP envelope, CDATA, comments, character references, non-ASCII names, mixed content) must pass, since a guard that refuses everything is not one. Every bound is driven with a document inside it and one outside; a hundred thousand levels of nesting must be refused by the depth bound rather than by the scanner recursing. The filter side checks that only the named media types and methods are read, that the body is replayed byte for byte, that an oversize document is refused rather than passed uninspected, and that report mode changes nothing but the log. Thirty-six deliberate weakenings were each caught, two after the tests were extended for them |
| `internal/filters/oidc` | `TestParse`, `TestSealOpen`, `TestRevocation` | Every option rule and default, no key file created by validation; seal and open with purpose binding, tampering and garbage refused, expired sessions refused, cookie stripping keeps other cookies, claim formatting |
| `internal/saml` | `TestCanonicalFormRendersOnlyTheNamespacesAnElementUses`, `TestCanonicalFormRendersAnInclusivePrefixList`, `TestTheParserRefusesWhatTheProfileRefuses`, `TestTheParserAcceptsWhatAProviderActuallySends`, `TestASignedAssertionLogsTheUserIn`, `TestASignedResponseLogsTheUserIn`, `TestTheRequiredSignatureIsTheOneThatMustBeThere`, `TestBothSignaturesVerifyTogether`, `TestAnECDSASignatureVerifies`, `TestAnECDSASignatureInTheWrongEncodingIsRefused`, `TestATruncatedSignatureIsRefusedRatherThanPanicking`, `TestAResponseOverTheBoundIsRefusedForItsSize`, `TestAnUnsignedResponseIsNeverALogin`, `TestAnAlteredAssertionDoesNotVerify`, `TestAnotherKeyDoesNotVerify`, `TestSignatureWrappingIsRefused`, `TestWeakAndOpenEndedSignatureOptionsAreRefused`, `TestAResponseMeantForSomebodyElseIsRefused`, `TestAnAuthnStatementIsRequired`, `TestAFailedStatusIsNotALogin`, `TestEncryptionIsRefusedWithAReasonThatSaysSo`, `TestAResponseNobodyAskedForIsNotALogin`, `TestAnAssertionIsAcceptedOnce`, `TestTheRequestSaysWhereTheAnswerGoes`, `TestTheMetadataNamesTheConsumerService`, `TestIdentityProviderMetadataIsRead`, `TestURLsAreComparedTheWayTheProfileMeans`, `TestTheDigestCoversTheWholeAssertion` | The canonical form is the one thing here that cannot be checked against this package's own output, because every signed fixture is produced with it and a wrong canonicalizer agrees with itself: the expected bytes are written out from the Exclusive XML Canonicalization rules by hand, and they caught the first real bug (the default namespace rendered on an element that did not use it). Fifteen documents the profile must refuse are driven through the parser, each asserted to fail *as a profile refusal* rather than by accident. Signature wrapping is attacked five ways -- a second assertion beside the signed one, the signed assertion hidden under an `Extensions` element with a forgery in its place, two elements sharing an `ID`, a signature whose reference names another element, and a broken signature on something nothing would read -- and all five are refused. Twenty-three single-field edits to a signed fixture are each refused with the error class they belong to, and five edits made *after* signing are each caught by the signature, which is what proves the digest covers the whole assertion. `TestTheDigestCoversTheWholeAssertion` reads the canonical form and asserts every attribute and value that matters is in it. | `internal/tui` (a real terminal) | `TestRunOverAPseudoTerminal` opens a pseudo terminal pair, hands the slave to `Run` and types at it: thirteen keys including the arrow and shift-tab sequences, the pause and refresh interval keys, an unban prompt that only `y` confirms, a ban prompt taking a *pasted* line with a backspace in the middle, an escape that cancels, and `q` which restores the screen it took over. Each key waits for the frame it draws rather than for a pause, which is both what makes it deterministic under load and what keeps the keys in separate reads; the pasted line is deliberately one write, because a read holding several keys is the case that was broken. `TestTheIntervalFetchesAndKeepsTheSelectionInRange` is the other half: the refresh interval fetching on its own, and the selection brought back in range when the list shrinks under the cursor. `TestSplitKeysCutsAReadIntoKeys` and `TestSplitKeysKeepsEveryByte` drive the cut itself -- a pasted address, a held key, cursor keys whole, an escape on its own, a truncated sequence, a multi-byte rune -- and assert no byte is invented or lost. `TestRunRefusesANonTerminal` | The one honest test of a program that takes over a terminal is a terminal |

The forwarded header is driven from a trusted peer, from an untrusted one, and with the setting off; a client's own header is beaten by the connection's certificate; and six malformed headers are each no certificate rather than a thumbprint of something else. The oversize case carries a *valid* twenty-kilobyte certificate, because that is the only way the bound is what refuses it. A token bound both ways is presented with the right key on the wrong connection and the right connection with no proof, so neither control can be hiding the other's failure. Eleven deliberate weakenings were each caught, one only after the oversize test was added |

| `internal/filters/samlsp` (flow) | `TestARequestWithoutASessionGoesToTheProvider`, `TestASignedAssertionSetsTheSession`, `TestAClientSuppliedIdentityHeaderNeverReachesTheUpstream`, `TestTheReturnURLCannotLeaveThisHost`, `TestTheSessionNeverOutlivesTheAssertion`, `TestAResponseNobodyCanUseTwice`, `TestTheConsumerServiceRefusesWhatItShould` (eleven, each with its own detail), `TestTheConsumerServiceTakesOnlyAPostedForm`, `TestAnAttributeRequirementIsEnforced`, `TestANameIdentifierFormatOutsideThePolicyIsRefused`, `TestASignatureOnTheResponseSatisfiesThatSetting`, `TestASessionSealedForAnotherProviderDoesNotOpen`, `TestTheMetadataEndpointDescribesThisServiceProvider`, `TestLogoutClearsTheSession`, `TestTheProviderCanBeNamedByItsMetadata`, `TestAnotherProvidersKeyIsNotThisProvidersKey`, `TestTheConfigurationIsCheckedAtLoad` (nineteen rejections, each by its own message), `TestTheDerivedConsumerURLNeedsATrustedPeer`, `TestTheStatusViewReportsWhatHappened` | The whole flow driven against a test identity provider (`internal/saml/samltest`) that signs real responses: the redirect is parsed as a provider would parse it, the request identifier it carried is what the response answers, and the session cookie it produces is what the next request is let through on. The refusal table covers an unsigned assertion, another audience, another consumer service, another login attempt, an expired assertion, a provider that refused, a tampered name identifier, a document that is not a response, a document type declaration, a missing state cookie and a relay state from another exchange -- each asserted to leave no identity behind. The return URL is driven with `//evil.example.com` and with four kilobytes of path, the forwarded identity header with a value the client chose and no attribute to overwrite it, and the derived consumer URL with `X-Forwarded-Proto` from an untrusted and then a trusted peer. Sixteen deliberate weakenings of the filter were each caught; two were not at first |
| `internal/jsonschema` (adversarial) | `TestReferenceCycle`, `TestSelfReference`, `TestRefChain`, `TestDanglingRef`, `TestExternalReferenceIsNotFetched` (a `$ref` naming a URL, a file or a relative path resolves to nothing — the XXE question asked of JSON), `TestPointerEscapes`, `TestDeepValueIsBounded`, `TestDeepSchemaIsBounded`, `TestReportIsBounded`, `TestIssueOrderIsStable`, `TestPatternIsNotBacktracking` (a nested quantifier against 40 characters: RE2 has no ReDoS, and this says so), `TestBadPatternIsIgnored`, `TestHugePatternIsBounded`, `TestRegexpCacheIsBounded`, `TestNumericEdges`, `TestNonFiniteNeverSatisfiesABound`, `TestCoerceRejectsNonJSONNumbers`, `TestCoerceArrayAndBoolean`, `TestStringSemantics` (lengths count runes, not bytes), `TestFormats` (every format with its near misses), `TestCombinators`, `TestObjectKeywords`, `TestArrayKeywords`, `TestUniqueItemsCostIsNotQuadratic` (20000 items), `TestNullHandling`, `TestEnumAndConst`, `TestDecodeIsStrict`, `TestDecodeKeepsNumberText`, `TestDuplicateKeysInBody`, `TestJsonify`, `TestLoadDocument`, `TestLoadDocumentDoesNotExpandAliases`, `TestConcurrentValidation`, `TestValidateIsPure`, `TestTypeAllows` | The validator that stands between an unauthenticated body and the origin: reference cycles and external references, recursion on both the value and the schema side, algorithmic complexity, the numbers and nulls a bound has to survive, and the properties a shared validator relies on |
| `internal/config` (adversarial) | `TestEntityExpansion` (the YAML billion laughs: nine anchors each referencing the previous nine times, refused in milliseconds), `TestAliasFanOutIsBounded`, `TestNestingDepth` (1000, 10000 and 100000 deep, no stack overflow), `TestUnclosedNesting`, `TestOversizeDocument`, `TestTruncation` (every prefix of a valid document), `TestTypeConfusion`, `TestYAMLBooleanLookalikes` (`no` stays a string), `TestNumericSemantics` (leading zero, explicit octal, hex, underscores, int64 overflow, NaN, Inf, decimal comma, exponent), `TestDurationSemantics`, `TestEmptyDurationMeansDefault`, `TestDuplicateKeys`, `TestUnknownFieldsRefused`, `TestEncodingHazards` (BOM, CRLF, lone CR, NUL, UTF-16, invalid UTF-8, lone surrogate, tab indentation), `TestControlCharactersInNames`, `TestMultipleDocuments`, `TestEmptyAndWhitespaceOnly`, `TestWhitespaceInValues`, `TestLongValues`, `TestManyItems` (20000 routes parse and validate with none dropped), `TestDeterministicErrors` (twenty runs produce byte-identical error text), `TestIdempotentParse`, `TestConcurrentParse` | The configuration parser against inputs nobody wrote on purpose: expansion and recursion budgets, truncation, type confusion, the numbers and booleans YAML disagrees about, encodings, and the properties an operator relies on — deterministic errors, no silent drops, no shared state |
| `internal/securitytxt` | `TestRender` (field order, comment block, verbatim body with CRLF and lone CR normalised), `TestExpires` (explicit RFC 3339 and `valid_for`, normalised to UTC), `TestMatch` (exact host, `*.suffix` not matching the bare name, regular expression, client CIDR, listener, first entry wins, nil set), `TestServe` (content type, `Cache-Control`, HEAD without a body, 405 for every other method), `TestNew` (a bad regular expression, a bad CIDR and a bad expiry are errors) | RFC 9116 rendering, expiry arithmetic, selector precedence and the HTTP surface |
| `internal/securitytxt` (selection by address) | `TestHostCIDRSelectsAddressLiterals` (sixteen `Host` values: both ends of a range and just outside it on each side, IPv6 bracketed as it travels, a mapped address matching the IPv4 prefix it carries, a name never matching however it resolves, and four things that look like addresses and are not), `TestHostCIDRCanCoverEveryLiteral` (`0.0.0.0/0` with `::/0` is every literal, and a name still falls through), `TestHostCIDRJoinsTheOtherHostSelectors` (one entry naming hosts, a wildcard, a regular expression and a range answers for any of them and for nothing else), `TestHostCIDRAndClientCIDRAreSeparateTests` (the host range and the client range are two questions about one request and both must hold), `TestHostCIDRCompileErrors` (four values that are not CIDRs, and a prefix with host bits set masked rather than refused); `internal/kinds/http` `TestSecurityTxtByHostAddress` drives the whole path through a real server, where the bracketed IPv6 spelling and the `:port` only exist on the wire | The host a finder reaches by address is the one with no name to go on, and the one a name-based selector cannot express |
| `internal/tlsconf` | `TestServer`, `TestClient` | SNI selection, hardening flags, insecure suite rejection, double opt-in |
| `internal/tlsconf` (certificate expiry) | `TestAnExpiredCertificateIsRefusedAtLoad`, `TestAReloadCannotInstallAnExpiredCertificate`, `TestExpiryWarningsAreReportedWorstFirst` | The three properties of `tls.expiry`, and the boundary between them. An expired certificate loads without the section, because serving one is not a security hole; with `refuse_expired` it does not, and the message names the *file* rather than the subject, since the file is what has to be replaced. Then the case the section exists for: a renewal that wrote an expired certificate over a working one is refused on reload and **the certificate in use is untouched**, asserted by comparing the served expiry before and after. The warnings are not refusals: two certificates inside the window are reported worst first and go on being served, a time past both reports two expiries and still serves, and `warn: 0` reports nothing *even for a certificate that has already expired* -- the case that tells "no window" apart from a window that happens to exclude everything |
| `internal/mgmt`, `internal/proxy` (readiness) | `TestSteppingANodeDownAndBackUp`, `TestTheJudgementCallsAreOptIn`, `TestTheVerdictReadsAsASentence` | The verdict a VRRP check script acts on. A step-down refuses with the operator's own note on it, is timed, and reverses; a reason past the field is refused rather than truncated; and a stepped-down node answers the *client* with a verdict although the endpoint reports 503 -- a client that mistook that for an unreachable daemon would move an address for the wrong reason, which is why this is the one call in `internal/mgmt` that treats a non-2xx as an answer. Then the two opt-in judgements, on one node so the only difference is what the operator decided matters: with neither, a drained pool and a hardening mechanism that did not apply are *reported while the node goes on serving*, because that is the state an alert is for; with either flag on, its own fault becomes the refusal |
| `internal/kinds/http` (rate limits over a reload) | `TestARateLimitSurvivesAReload`, `TestWhatCountsAsTheSameRateLimit` | A limit is state. The test spends a burst at a rate so low that nothing refills within the test, so anything allowed after the reload came from a bucket that was thrown away; the same configuration keeps the bucket, and a *changed* rate starts again, because a level measured in the old rate's tokens says nothing about the new one. The shape comparison is then checked field by field in both directions: the action, the tarpit delay and the cluster semantics are about the decision and keep the buckets, while the rate, burst, algorithm, limit, window, key and both prefix lengths are about the counting and must reset them |

| `internal/filters/formguard` | `TestHiddenField`, `TestBodyIsReplayed`, `TestOtherEncodingsPass`, `TestTiming`, `TestRequireFetch`, `TestFormPathPrefix`, `TestMethods`, `TestEvict`, `TestOptionsAreValidated` | The hidden field: an honest submission passes, a field that arrives empty (or as whitespace, which is what a browser sends) passes, a filled one is denied with `field:<name>`, and the same field smuggled in the query string of the submission is caught too. The body the application receives is byte for byte what the client sent, a JSON body on the same route is not this filter's business, and a body past `max_body_bytes` passes uninspected rather than being refused for its size. The clock: no fetch on record is allowed, a GET of a form path is remembered, a submission on its heels is `too_fast` with `form_seconds` in the attributes, a paced one passes, a page fetched two days ago is `too_old`, and timing is judged before the fields. `require_fetch` refuses with `no_form_fetch` and its own status and reason until a fetch exists. A configured form path matches with and without its trailing slash and does not match a longer sibling; a filter that names only PUT ignores a POST. A full fetch table sweeps rather than grows, and the client whose fetch it forgot is allowed, not refused. And the options table: a filter that checks nothing, a field name with a space, a range, a minimum past the maximum, timing without `form_paths`, a path without a leading slash, GET as a submission method, a 200 and a 503 as the refusal, bodies and tables out of bounds, and an unknown key |
| `internal/dns` (DoQ, SVCB, discovery) | `TestDoQEndToEnd`, `TestDoQMalformed`, `TestDoQWrongALPN`, `TestDoQUpstream`, `TestParseQUICUpstream`; `TestSVCBRoundTrip`, `TestSVCBParamsRefused`, `TestSVCBMalformed`, `TestDiscoveryRecords`, `TestLocalRecordsAnswered` | A real QUIC client against a real DoQ listener: the answer comes back with the zero id RFC 9250 requires, the counters move, and a second connection still works. The stream parser is driven with no length, half a length, a length with no message, a message shorter than a header, a zero length and a truncated message, and must answer none of them. A client offering the `h3` ALPN is refused, since the ALPN is the only thing separating DoQ from HTTP/3 on one port. The resolver speaks DoQ to an upstream, restores the client's id, and reuses the connection for a second query. Records: parameters round-trip through the presentation form an operator would recognise, the encoding is independent of the order they were written in (RFC 9460's canonical form), and every malformed shape is refused — truncation inside a parameter, parameters out of order or repeated, an alias record carrying parameters, a port that is not one, an address hint of the wrong family, base64 that is not, a `dohpath` without its template variable. Discovery builds the records for each transport with the right ALPN and priority order. End to end: an owned name is answered locally with the AA bit and the ECH parameter in it, the discovery name answers, a type the name does not have gets NOERROR with no answers rather than a forwarded lookup, and a name the resolver does not own still goes upstream |
| `internal/kinds/http` (WebSocket) | `TestWebSocketGuardPasses`, `TestWebSocketGuardCloses`, `TestWebSocketGuardServerSide`, `TestWebSocketGuardLogAction`, `TestWebSocketSubprotocol`, `TestWebSocketRate`, `TestWebSocketNoGuardIsUntouched`, `TestWSCloseCodes` | A real upgrade against an origin that speaks RFC 6455, with the frames built by hand in the test. An ordinary conversation — short and long messages, a ping, a fragmented message — passes untouched, which a guard that broke normal traffic would fail first. Then the structural table, one connection per case: an unmasked client frame, a reserved bit, a reserved opcode, a frame over the bound, a fragmented control frame, an oversize control frame, a continuation with nothing to continue, a close code that must not be sent, a one byte close payload, a denied pattern and text that is not UTF-8 — each closed with a close frame carrying a truthful code rather than a reset. The origin's own messages are inspected too, so a compromised application cannot push what the client may not send. `action: log` records the violation and forwards the message unchanged, including through the write path where a short write would otherwise abort the copy. The subprotocol is refused on the 101 before any frame exists; a flood is cut off by the rate bound; a route without the section is the opaque tunnel it always was; and the close code table is checked against the RFC and the registry in both directions |
| `internal/upstream` (Consul discovery) | `TestConsulUsesBlockingQueries`, `TestConsulFollowsTheService`, `TestConsulQueryCarriesTagDatacenterAndToken`, `TestConsulRefusesAnUnreadableTokenFile`, `TestConsulDoesNotSpinOnAFailure`, `TestConsulResetsAnIndexThatWentBackwards` | Against a stand-in agent that carries an index and records whether each query was a blocking one, because "it blocks" is the whole reason for the type and a polling implementation would pass every other test. An instance that goes away leaves the pool and a new one joins it; the tag, datacenter and token reach the agent, with the token trimmed of the newline a file ends with; an unreadable token file fails at build rather than on the first query. Then the two failure modes that matter more than the happy path: a dead agent must be retried on the interval rather than in a tight loop -- asserted by counting queries against a closed server -- and must not empty the pool; and an index that goes backwards, which a Consul server restart produces, must not leave the proxy blocking on an index that will never be reached |
| `internal/kinds/ssh` (the certificate policy and the bounds) | `TestACertificateRestrictedToAnotherNetworkIsRefused`, `TestACertificateGrantsOnlyWhatItCarries`, `TestACertificateWithoutPermitPtyGetsNoTerminal`, `TestForceCommandRunsInsteadOfWhatWasAsked`, `TestACertificateLongerThanTheListenerAcceptsIsRefused`, `TestRevocationOverridesTheAuthority`, `TestShellOperatorsAreRefusedBeforeThePatterns`, `TestMaxSessionsPerPrincipal`, `TestMaxForwards` | Each case signs its own certificate, which is what lets the CA's restrictions be varied one at a time. source-address is driven three ways -- a network this connection is not on, the network it is on, and a list the gateway cannot parse, which must refuse rather than be ignored. The extensions are driven in both directions: a certificate with only `permit-pty` gets its terminal and is refused the port forward the listener would otherwise allow, and one with only `permit-port-forwarding` is refused the terminal. force-command is asserted against what the target saw: the forced command arrived and what the client asked for did not. The lifetime bound refuses a week and a certificate that never ends and accepts an hour. The header is 48 octets and the packet is not: a tail of extension fields, a MAC, both or neither, and RFC 7822 cannot always tell the last field from a MAC -- so the reading is the one every implementation makes (a 20 or 24-octet tail is a MAC) and the packet says the reading was a choice, which a policy can then refuse. Before version 4 there are no extension fields at all, so a tail there can only be a MAC, and a fuzz target found the bug that made this worth pinning: the authenticated region was left at the whole packet rather than the header, which would have verified a MAC over bytes the MAC did not cover. The timestamp arithmetic is tested across the end of an era, where the wire values wrap and the protocol's own modular subtraction has to give milliseconds rather than a century; the fixed-point fields saturate rather than wrapping, because a wrapped root dispersion reads as an excellent clock. AES-CMAC is checked against RFC 4493's vectors with the subkeys checked separately, so a failure says which half is wrong, and the MAC verification is driven through a flipped digest bit, an edited packet, an unknown key identifier, a digest of the wrong length for the algorithm, the legacy algorithms and a crypto-NAK, which is a statement rather than a failure. The reference identifier is read by stratum -- a kiss code at 0, a reference clock's name at 1, and at 2 and above something that may be an address and may be four octets of a hash, which the rendering says rather than guessing. Then the policy as a unit, in both directions: the dispatch refuses modes 6 and 7 and version 5 from the first octet, the request rules cover the peer-only association modes, the field bounds, the two identities a listener can demand and the ambiguity, and the response rules are the server-quality ones -- unsynchronised by leap indicator and by stratum 16 as separate statements, the stratum, delay, dispersion and offset bounds, the kiss-o'-death as a message rather than time, and the two downgrades (NTS or a MAC asked for and not answered) that are refusals rather than a quieter answer. Then the relay end to end against a fake time server: an ordinary exchange whose answer carries the client's own transmit timestamp, so the client can verify it and the relay cannot be in the middle of the cryptography; management-mode packets that never reach a server, which is the amplification this port is famous for; the version profile including a legacy version named and an unnamed one refused; the rate limit answered with a RATE kiss rather than a drop; five quality refusals with nothing forwarded; an unsolicited answer from the right address dropped, because the origin timestamp is the only thing tying an answer to a question; interleaved mode forwarded and, with the switch off, dropped as what it cannot be told from; NTS passed through whole with the fields reaching the server and a stripped answer refused; a symmetric packet from something that is not a peer; the extension policy and the ambiguous tail; authentication with a good MAC, a bad one and none; the monitor finding the one source of three that lies by five seconds and separating a silent source from a wrong one; learning mode passing what it would have refused, not applying the client list it is being written against, and writing the three lists at shutdown; the trace in both directions; eight clients behind one address each getting its own answer; four malformed shapes that never reach a server; and an answer the relay cannot read, which is counted and refused as malformed rather than dropped in silence or handed to whoever asked last. Three rules are asserted on the bytes rather than on the decision: a signed request reaches the server as the client's own datagram and its answer reaches the client with the server's own digest still verifying, which is the case a relay that re-rendered the packet would silently break; the association table's bound drops the client past it and leaves the ones already there working; and the egress list refuses a server outside it when the socket is opened, before a packet is sent, with the same listener working once its network is named. The monitor gets the two halves a single-sample test cannot see: a source whose leap indicator says its own clock is not synchronised is a state of its own rather than an outage, and the transition waits for as many bad probes as the listener asked for. The key establishment relay is driven with a real TLS handshake through it: an NTS client completes one with the server behind the relay, a client offering the wrong application protocol or none is refused before the server sees it, a server name outside the list is refused, a plain HTTP request is refused as a scan, and both the connection bound and the handshake bound are exercised -- the handshake bound waiting on the `ntske_handshakes` gauge before the second client dials, because a dial that returns first is not a client that was served first, and asserting the gauge comes back down when the slot is given up. The ClientHello reader that makes that possible is tested on a real client's bytes and fuzzed, and the datagram relay's session clock has a test of its own: the activity stamp is an elapsed time rather than a wall-clock one, because this is the host whose clock the NTP gateway two listeners over is helping to set. Last, the checks added when the gateway stopped only measuring answers and started reading them. Against the packet itself: the synchronisation distance is both halves and each half can sit inside its own bound while the sum does not; a leap second is plausible only at the end of the four months the IERS uses, and the window is the caller's because implementations announce at different times; the four timestamps are read against each other and not against this relay's clock, including across the end of an era where the seconds field wraps and the modular subtraction has to see a millisecond, and a client's request is not judged as an answer at all; the reference identifier is read by its stratum, so a stratum 1 answer that names no reference clock is refused while four octets that could be a hash of an IPv6 address are not called wrong for looking like a multicast address. Through the policy: each of those refusals by name, the interleaved exemption (whose transmit timestamp is the server's previous one on purpose), the stratum list as distinct from the stratum bound, the expected reference identifiers, and the four leap policies including the default that forwards the announcement and says so. Then the watcher, which is about the server rather than the packet: the first measurement is a baseline and not a change, an unchanged server is not an alert per poll, each of the six changes is named once and not again (the baseline moves to what was measured), a kiss-o'-death and an unsynchronised answer never become the baseline so the next real answer is not a change, NTS stopping is a change and NTS starting is not, the thresholds are the operator's, off means off, the table is bounded, and a nil watcher is safe on every method. End to end, through a real relay whose test server changes what it is halfway through: a new reference identifier and a stratum jump are both counted while every static bound is generous, the same change with `action: refuse` drops that one answer and then answers again because the change has become the new normal, a leap second out of season is forwarded and counted, and an answer whose timestamps cannot describe an exchange is refused by that reason rather than as unsolicited |

Octets pipelined behind `AUTH TLS` end the session rather than being read as if they had arrived inside it. A listener with no certificate answers 534 and a mechanism that is not TLS 504. With `upstream_tls_mode: starttls` the target's leg goes first, so a target that will not protect the connection is found out before the client is told its own is protected — 431, against a target that answers AUTH with 534. A client outside `allow_clients` is never greeted and never reaches the target; the connection over `max_connections` gets 421. The target is sent a PROXY v2 header carrying the client's own port, and reads it unconditionally rather than sniffing for one. A target that is not there is 421, not a hung connection. `{user}` in `allow_paths` is a per-user home directory, and a login that cannot be put in a path ends the session rather than being substituted into one. YARA over an upload cuts the transfer before the marked content reaches the target, and a clean upload through the same policy still arrives |
