The HTTP filter is driven end to end — challenge page, posted code, signed cookie, request through — and then adversarially: a cookie is worthless on another identity and worthless when tampered with, a wrong code, a replayed one and an unenrolled name produce byte-identical pages, a request with no identity is refused rather than prompted, and an off-site redirect target becomes `/`. On the bastion the same factor is driven over a real SSH client: the key alone opens nothing, the wrong code opens nothing and reaches no target, the same code cannot open a second session, and an unenrolled user is asked before being refused, so nothing says which names are enrolled |
| `internal/mfa` (administration), `internal/proxy`, `internal/mgmt`, `internal/admin`, `internal/tui` (second factors an operator changes) | `TestEnrolMakesAWorkingFactor`, `TestRemoveTakesEffectAtOnce`, `TestAnEditOutsideIsPickedUp`, `TestABrokenFileChangesNothing`, `TestNamesAreChecked`, `TestRecoveryCodesAreReplaced`, `TestParametersSurviveTheFile`, `TestTheFileStaysPrivate`, `TestTheStatusViewShowsNoSecret`, `TestRecoveryCodesAreLegible`, `TestForgettingAUserClearsTheState`; `TestEnrolThroughTheSocketWorksImmediately`, `TestRemoveThroughTheSocket`, `TestRecoveryThroughTheSocket`, `TestUnlockThroughTheSocket`, `TestAListenerWithoutAFactorIsRefused`, `TestABadNameIsRefused`; `TestMFAListingIsReadableByAViewer`, `TestMFAEnrolmentIsPassedOnUntouched`, `TestMFAActionsForwardAndAudit`, `TestMFARequestsThatAreNotRequests`; `TestMFAViewAndKeys` | The file is the authority, so the tests are about what happens to a live listener when it changes: an enrolment written through the socket authenticates on the next connection without a reload, a removal stops authenticating, replaced recovery codes retire the old ones, and an unlock forgives guessing wrong without forgiving replay — a code spent before the lockout is still spent. The writes are checked for the things that lose every second factor at once: a file edited by hand in between is not undone, a file that becomes unreadable changes nothing in force, the replacement keeps mode 0600, and a name the format could not hold is refused before anything is written. A name enrolled again does not inherit the last holder's lockout, and recovery codes come from an alphabet without the characters people read back wrongly. Through the socket, a listener that asks for no factor is refused by name rather than silently doing nothing, and the lockout a test drives by failing six telnet logins is read back from the status document. In the GUI the listing reaches a viewer (it holds no secret) while all four changes refuse one, the names are trimmed and bounded before they reach the socket, the enrolment answer — secret, URI and recovery codes — is passed through untouched, a refusal from the data plane arrives with its own message, and a request without the cross-site header is refused before any of that. In the TUI the screen shows who is locked out and nothing that could authenticate anybody, the cursor cannot run off the end, changing screen puts it back at the top so a key cannot act on an unrelated row, each action asks first, and a refusal is reported rather than swallowed |
| `internal/config`, `internal/kinds/ftp` (seventh round sweep) | `TestEveryReasonAKindEmitsCanBeBannedOn`; `TestARecoveryCodeOpensTheSession` | The sixth round's findings, swept across the kinds beside the one each was found on, and what the sweep turned up. The ban-reason test reads the source of every listener kind for the reason it hands the ban list and requires that a trigger can name it, rather than keeping a second list beside the first -- which is how four kinds came to report a refusal no configuration could ban on, documented in CONFIG.md and the man page the whole time. It fails for a kind added tomorrow until its reason is nameable. The FTP test is the half of the recovery-code fix the RDP half already had: a real enrolment written by the control plane, its first recovery code carried on the password where a seventeen-character code used to be one too long to be seen as one, the target checked never to have received it, and the same code refused on a second session because it is single use |
| `internal/rdp`, `internal/rfb`, `internal/ntlm`, `internal/qr`, `internal/mfa`, `internal/kinds/rdp` (sixth audit round) | `FuzzReadPDU`, `FuzzParseConnect`, `FuzzParseChannels`, `FuzzParseServerChannels`, `FuzzParseClientInfo`, `FuzzParseDeviceAnnounce`, `FuzzParseServerSecurity`, `FuzzParseSendData`, `FuzzParseChannelChunk`, `FuzzParseConnectionRequest`, `FuzzParseSecurityExchange`, `FuzzReadTSRequest`, `TestAShortLengthIndicatorIsNotASlice`; `FuzzReadSecurityList`, `FuzzReadString`, `FuzzReadServerInit`, `FuzzReadSubtypes`, `FuzzReadTightCapabilities`, `FuzzReadRSAAESKey`, `FuzzReadARDParams`, `FuzzReadMSLogonParams`, `FuzzReadVersions`, `FuzzReadPlain`, `FuzzReadRSAAESCredential`, `FuzzReadTightInteraction`, `FuzzReadSecurityResult`; `FuzzParseChallenge`; `FuzzEncode`; `TestARecoveryCodeFitsWhereACodeGoes`, `TestAWrongCodeCostsTheSameWhoeverItIsFor`; `TestADesktopCannotMakeTheGatewaySkipTheCredential`, `TestARecoveryCodeOpensTheDesktop` | The sixth round, over what was added after the fifth: the RDP connection sequence and both of its encryption layers, the RFB handshake with the vendors' own security types, NTLM, CredSSP, the QR encoder and the second factors the control plane can change. Twenty-seven fuzz targets at around five million executions each, holding the invariant the earlier rounds used -- a parser may refuse anything, but may not panic, may not allocate on a length it has not checked, and what it returns must be inside what arrived. They found a remotely reachable panic: an X.224 length indicator shorter than the header it covers made the options slice one whose start was past its end, on the first packet of a connection and in the mirror parser that reads a desktop's answer. Beside them, the things fuzzing cannot see. A desktop that leaves the server network block out of its conference response used to leave the gateway watching channel zero, so the credential went through with no factor checked -- the test drives exactly that desktop and asserts nothing reaches it. A recovery code is seventeen characters and the two protocols that carry a code inside a password would split off sixteen, so the recovery path did not work on either; the test drives a real generated code through an RDP session end to end, and pins the length against what the generator makes. And a wrong code used to cost a second of processor time for an enrolled name against twenty microseconds for an unenrolled one, which is both an enumeration oracle and a denial of service; the test asserts the cost is bounded and that the two are within a factor of eight, measured best-of-five so a loaded machine does not make it flap |
| `internal/qr` | `TestAgainstTheReferenceSymbols`, `TestErrorCorrectionMatchesTheStandardsExample`, `TestCapacities`, `TestVersionIsTheSmallestThatFits`, `TestRefusals`, `TestFunctionPatterns`, `TestPNG`, `TestEveryMaskIsSelectable` | A QR encoder is the kind of code that runs, produces a plausible square, and is wrong: nothing in the output looks broken until somebody's telephone refuses it. So the test is 640 symbols — every version from 1 to 40, every one of the eight masks, two payload sizes each — compared by hash against symbols made by an implementation with nothing to do with this one (`testdata/reference.txt`, whose provenance is in its header). A wrong block count, a misplaced alignment pattern, a version information word off by a bit or a nearly right generator polynomial each change the grid and none of them would change whether the code runs. Beside that: the error correction codewords of ISO/IEC 18004's own worked example, so the field and the division are pinned to the standard rather than to another implementation; the codeword capacities, which this package counts from its own layout instead of a table; the smallest version that fits, checked one byte either side of all 39 boundaries; the finders, timing patterns and the module that is always dark, because a symbol wrong there is not found at all; and the PNG, pixel for module, with its `data:` URI small enough to sit in a page |
| `internal/yara`, `internal/filters/yarascan`, `internal/kinds/tcp` (YARA) | `TestLiteralStrings`, `TestModifiers`, `TestHexStrings`, `TestRegexStrings`, `TestConditions`, `TestStreamingAcrossWrites`, `TestFiresEarly`, `TestCompileRefuses`, `TestCommentsAndMeta`, `TestPrivateRule`, `TestEscapes`, `FuzzCompile`; `TestBlocksRequestBody`, `TestPassesCleanBody`, `TestBlocksResponseBody`, `TestLogAction`, `TestScanRequestOnly`, `TestContentTypes`, `TestPartialScan`, `TestOptionsRefused`; `TestYARAClosesOnMatch`, `TestYARAPassesClean`, `TestYARAScansUpstream`, `TestYARALogOnly`, `TestYARAOneDirection`, `TestYARABadRulesFail`; `TestYARARules` | The language first: literals with each modifier that changes what they match (wide must not match the narrow form, fullword must not match inside a word), hex with `??` and nibble wildcards and a bounded jump, regular expressions, and a table of eighteen conditions each against an input that should and should not satisfy it. Then the refusals, which are the point of a subset: eighteen rule files that must **not** compile, among them a module reference, `at`, a `for` loop, an unbounded jump, hex alternation, `@a`, an unknown modifier, a duplicate rule and a count that can never be true. A fuzz target holds the claim that anything that compiles also scans without panicking. The streaming behaviour has its own two tests, because it is where a stream scanner differs from a file scanner: a pattern split across two writes is still found, and a rule fires before the stream ends rather than at Close. The filter is driven through block and log on both directions, a body past the bound forwarded whole and marked partial, content types narrowing what is looked at, and seven option sets that must not build. On a real layer 4 listener the connection is cut on a match with the rest never reaching the upstream, a clean stream passes through byte for byte, the upstream direction is scanned too, `action: log` forwards while counting, a listener told to watch one direction does not scan the other, and a rule set naming a module fails the listener rather than starting without it. Finally the example rule set is compiled and each rule checked against something it claims to match and against ordinary traffic that must trip none of them |
| `internal/kinds/forward` (SOCKS5) | `TestSOCKS5Connect`, `TestSOCKS5Policy`, `TestSOCKS5Auth`, `TestSOCKS5Malformed`, `TestSOCKS5UDP`, `TestSOCKS5UDPOffByDefault`, `TestSOCKSUDPHeaderParsing` | The handshake is written out byte by byte in the test rather than taken from a library, so what is exercised is the wire format. A client reaches an origin through the tunnel while the HTTP proxy on the same port keeps working, which is the demultiplexer's whole claim. The destination policy answers with truthful reply codes — connection-not-allowed for a denied name and an unlisted port, host-unreachable for a name that does not resolve. Credentials share the users file with the HTTP side: the right password passes, a wrong one and an unknown user are refused identically, and a client offering only "no authentication" gets `0xFF`. Then the malformed table: an empty greeting, no methods, a truncated method list, SOCKS4, a wrong version, a truncated request, an empty domain name, a name with a NUL, an address type nobody has, and BIND — each must close without ever sending a success reply. UDP: a datagram out and its answer back through a real echo server, the answer arriving from the relay port, and an unsolicited datagram from a third host dropped rather than delivered, which is what stops the socket being an open reflector; associations are refused when `socks_udp` is off; and the datagram header parser is driven through short, reserved-set, fragmented, unknown-type, truncated-IPv6, empty-name, over-long-name and slash-in-name cases |
| `internal/ech`, `internal/tlsconf` (ECH), `internal/config` | `TestRoundTrip`, `TestGoAcceptsOurConfig`, `TestListRoundTrip`, `TestMalformed`, `TestMandatoryExtension`, `TestPublicNameRules`; `TestECHAccepted`, `TestECHFallbackStillWorks`, `TestECHRequireRefuses`, `TestECHStaleKeyIsRetried`, `TestECHKeyMismatchIsRefusedAtLoad`; `TestECHValidation`, `TestECHKeyFormats` | The encoder and decoder agree, and crypto/tls — which has its own parser and is the one that matters — accepts what we generate. Every truncation of a config is refused, along with a wrong length, an unknown KEM, another version (named, not guessed at), trailing bytes, an oversize config, an empty list, and a mandatory extension this build does not implement. Then real handshakes: a client that learned the config encrypts its hello and the inner name never leaves the machine, while the status counts it; a client that learned nothing falls back to the public name and is counted as such; `require` refuses that fallback on the first read, which is what its clients would see; a client holding a stale config is handed the current one in the rejection, so a rotation heals itself; and a config served with the wrong private key is refused at load rather than falling back silently forever. Validation covers a duplicate config id, a world-readable key, a public name no certificate covers, `require`'s advice line, and a junk config file; the loader accepts a raw config, base64, a pasted `ech="..."` record, and raw, base64 and hex keys |
| `internal/tlsconf` (key exchange) | `TestDefaultIsPostQuantum`, `TestClassicalClientStillConnects`, `TestConfiguredListIsTheAcceptedSet`, `TestUnknownGroupIsRefused`, `TestUpstreamDefaultsToHybrid`, `TestGroupNames`; `internal/config` `TestKeyExchangeValidation` | Real handshakes against a listener built from the configuration: the default negotiates X25519MLKEM768, which is the whole point — Go only offers the hybrid while `CurvePreferences` is unset, and this package sets it. A client that offers only classical groups still connects and is not counted as post-quantum; a listener that accepts one group forces it through a retry, and one that accepts several lets the client's key share decide. An unknown group name is refused on a listener and towards an upstream, and the upstream default is the hybrid too, with and without a `tls` section. Names round-trip both ways and a group this build does not know is still named in a log. Validation: a typo is refused with the known groups listed, a duplicate is refused, and a classical-only list loads with advice about what was traded, while the default list produces none |
| `internal/icap` | `TestOptionsAndReqmod`, `TestRespmodAndErrors`, `TestUnreachable`, `TestHeaderBlocks` | Against a fake ICAP server (`icaptest`): OPTIONS parsing, preview then `100 Continue` then `204`, block on preview with the encapsulated page, small and empty bodies, modified request, connection reuse, RESPMOD clean, blocked and rewritten, server error and timeout counted with recovery, unreachable service, header block rendering without hop-by-hop or injected headers |
| `internal/metrics` | `TestOTLPExporter`, `TestEncoder`, `TestHistogram`, `TestSeriesAndSampler` | OTLP/JSON push against a fake collector: gzip, headers, resource attributes, sums with attributes and start time, gauges, histogram bucket counts with the overflow bucket, status, the interval loop, a failing collector counted, a final push on stop, a missing CA refused; Text format with escaping and sorted labels, histogram buckets, sum and count; atomic histogram bucketing; ring buffer order, retention, since and limit, per-second rates from counters, counter reset handling, start and stop |
| `internal/mgmt` (capture) | `TestCaptureAPI`, `TestCaptureAPIWithoutASection` | Over the socket: a configured section reports itself enabled, idle and carrying its rules; a start opens a window of the length asked for; a window longer than `max_duration` is shortened to it rather than refused; a stop clears both the flag and the deadline; a duration that is not one is refused with a message naming it and leaves the state as it was; and a proxy with no capture section reports the section missing and refuses to start one |
| `internal/kinds/http` (deceive) | `TestDeceiveOnlyTheDistrusted`, `TestDeceivedWritesNeverReachTheOrigin`, `TestDeceiveNarrowing` | Mostly about who does *not* get the lie: on a route that deceives marked clients, an ordinary client reaches the origin and is not counted, a client that read a decoy gets the configured body with the configured type and the origin is never asked, and a third client on the same route is served normally. Nothing in the deceptive answer identifies it as one. A deceived POST is discarded with a 200 the origin never saw. And the narrowing: the named network with an unnamed method reaches the origin, the named method gets the decoy body, and a route without a `deceive` block is untouched |
| `internal/kinds/http` (degradation) | `TestDegradationShapesTheMarked`, `TestDegradationRateIsReal`, `TestDegradationDelayHoldsTheResponse`, `TestDegradationFirstLevelWins` | An unmarked client is untouched — full speed, keep-alive intact, nothing counted — and the same client, once it has read a decoy, gets the real body on a connection the transport reports as not reusable. A 64 KiB response through a 32 KiB/s level arrives whole and takes the time the rate implies, against an unshaped control; a level with a delay holds the response and a clean request beside it is not held; and with a narrow level above a wide one the narrow one takes the request |
| `internal/kinds/http` (handshake) | `TestHandshakeRefusesADeniedFingerprint`, `TestHandshakeRefusesAFingerprintPrefix`, `TestHandshakeRefusesABannedClient`, `TestHandshakeWithoutAPolicy` | Against a real TLS listener, with the test learning its own JA4 from the proxy that just saw it (the fingerprint table is keyed by the connection's address, which the dialer reports): the exact fingerprint on the deny list fails to negotiate, its prefix form refuses the same client, and the reason never reaches the client — a handshake alert carries no message. A client that earns a ban by reading a decoy gets no handshake on its next connection. Without a `handshake` section the hook refuses nothing and the status reports no policy |
| `internal/kinds/http` (honeytokens) | `TestHoneytokenFindsThePlantWhereverItComesBack`, `TestHoneytokenIgnoresOrdinaryTraffic`, `TestHoneytokenContainsMatch`, `TestHoneytokenFieldNarrowing`, `TestHoneytokenWorkIsBounded`, `TestHoneytokenValuesFile`, `TestHoneytokenCountersSurviveAReload`, `TestHoneytokenEndToEnd`, `TestHoneytokenValueIsNotLogged` | The two ways a tripwire fails. It has to fire wherever the plant comes back: a bare header, a bearer token, a lower-case scheme, either half of a Basic credential, a cookie, a query parameter encoded or not, a path and a path segment. And it must not fire on anything else: a real-looking key, a prefix of the token, the token with a character appended, the token inside a longer value (for an exact plant), an ordinary page, a session cookie. Then the narrowing (`in` and `headers`), `contains` against `exact` on the same value, a values file with comments and blanks and the refusal of a value too short to be one, counters that survive a reload, and the bounds that keep a client from choosing how much work the check does. End to end: ordinary traffic untouched, the plant refused with the configured status before the origin, the client marked with the token as its route and banned by a threshold-of-one trigger, a `log` token served and counted — and no log line anywhere carrying the value, only the name |
| `internal/capture` | `TestPad`, `TestOptionSkipsEmpty`, `TestHeaderIsAWholeFile`, `TestPacketTimestampAndTruncation`, `TestWriterReportsFailure`, `TestFlowIsAConversationADissectorAccepts`, `TestFlowSplitsAtTheMTU`, `TestFlowEmptyDirectionWritesNothing`, `TestFlowIPv6`, `TestFlowMixedFamiliesAreCoherent`, `TestChecksumRFC1071`, `TestNewDisabledIsNil`, `TestNoRulesMeansEveryExchange`, `TestRuleSelectors`, `TestAnswerSelectorsAreRetrospective`, `TestFirstMatchingRuleDecides`, `TestMaxFlowsBoundsARule`, `TestPercentSamples`, `TestInactiveCapturerWritesNothing`, `TestWindowClosesItself`, `TestSetActiveClampsToMaxDuration`, `TestCarryFromKeepsTheWindow`, `TestFileIsPrivateAndParsable`, `TestRotationAndPruning`, `TestWriteFailureIsCountedNotLogged`, `TestTruncationIsCounted`, `TestCommentSanitises`, `TestMaxBodyAndRedactReportTheConfiguration`, `TestBadRuleIsRefusedAtBuild`, `TestConcurrentWritesProduceOneWholeFile` | The pcapng files are read back by a parser written for the tests, so every assertion is about the file a pcap tool would open: block framing with the length repeated at both ends, the byte order magic, the section header and interface block that begin every file including a rotated one, microsecond timestamps, the original length of a truncated frame and the request id as a per-packet comment. The synthesised conversation is parsed as Ethernet, IP and TCP and checked as a dissector would: the handshake, data and close in order, IPv4 header and TCP checksums that verify over the pseudo-header, the IP length agreeing with the frame, sequence numbers advancing by the payload with one consumed by SYN and by FIN, a body split at the MTU that reassembles to what was written, an IPv6 conversation, a mixed-family pairing written coherently as IPv6, and RFC 1071 itself. Then the capturer: a disabled section yields a nil every method tolerates, no rules means every exchange, each selector matching and missing, the answer selectors holding the request until there is an answer, the first matching rule deciding, `max_flows` bounding a rule without over-counting, sampling, an idle capturer recording nothing, a window that closes itself, a duration clamped to `max_duration`, a reload carrying the window over unchanged, a file created `0600` under the configured prefix, rotation and pruning to `max_files`, a write failure counted rather than logged, truncation counted and marked in the comment, control characters and length bounded out of a comment, and two hundred concurrent exchanges producing one file whose every block still frames |
| `internal/shed` | `TestInflightLevel`, `TestLatencyLevelAndDrain`, `TestReconfigureKeepsSamples` | Class thresholds against the in-flight ratio, hysteresis, latency level from windowed samples, drain after an idle window, reconfiguration |
| `internal/challenge` | `TestFlow`, `TestVerifyInputs`, `TestPersistentKey`, `TestKeyRotation`, `TestProofDefinition`, `TestScriptSHA256MatchesGo`, `TestTiersAndDevice`, `TestCaptcha` | Page and headers, proof verification, wrong proof and wrong address refused, replay refused, cookie bound to address, expiry and tampering, exemptions, method and input validation, expired nonces, open redirect neutralised, key persistence and rotation, proof definition and form fields shared with the script; proof, captcha, legacy and device-less cookies read back with their tier and device, a forged tier refused, device parsing, the posted device landing in the cookie, devices off; against a fake provider: escalation and always modes, widget, script and policy origins per provider, rejected token, low score, unreachable provider, a pass with the secret, token and address sent, nonce single use, proofs alongside, statistics, missing and empty secrets, hostname binding refusing a token solved for another host, an allowlist overriding the request host and the check turned off |
| `internal/waf` | `TestBlockSQLi`, `TestDetectMode`, `TestCleanRequestPasses`, `TestBodyInspectionAndReplay`, `TestBodyLimitReject`, `TestResponseInspection`, `TestCustomDirectivesAndBadRules`, `TestOnlyNeededModesCompiled`, `TestRuleStatistics`, `TestLearningProposals`, `TestLearningTableBound`, `TestQuoteTarget`, `TestCRSDirectory`, `TestCRSDirectoryErrors`, `TestProfilesWithoutCRS`, `TestCRSPlugins`, `TestJSONSchemaBlock`, `TestJSONSchemaRequired`, `TestAnomalyDetection`, `TestAnomalyTrackerBound` | CRS blocks injection in query and body, detect mode logs without denying, clean traffic produces no attributes, inspected bodies are replayed intact, 413 above the body limit, response leakage blocked and clean or oversize responses pass intact, custom SecLang rules, compile errors surface, lazy compilation per mode; per rule counters with severity, tags, ordering, top bound and reset; learning proposals below and at `min_hits` with clients, path scoping, unique ids and a global form, the rendered file, and the proposed directives compiled into a new engine stop the same request without leaking outside the path; the entry bound and dropped count; target quoting; a copy of the embedded CRS loaded from a directory blocks injection, reports its source and version, picks up an added rule file and a preferred `crs-setup.conf`, fails on a broken file; missing, file, no setup and no rules directories refused; profiles without the CRS report and learn from custom rules; plugins as loose files and as a checked out repository with a before rule reading a data file and an after rule, selection by name, a missing name and an empty directory refused; JSON body schemas: a valid body replayed, a violation denied with a problem body and attributes, malformed JSON, 413 over the limit, other paths, methods and media types untouched, detect mode logging, the violation counter, `required` refusing a missing body and a wrong media type, a missing schema file; ### Integration tests (in the kind packages)

These start a full `Server` on loopback with real `httptest` backends and
drive it with `net/http` and raw TCP.

A listener kind's tests live with the kind, in `internal/kinds/<kind>`,
and start the same server through `internal/proxytest` — a kind is
tested the way it is deployed, through a real engine over a real
socket, not through a constructor of its own. The HTTP data plane is a
kind like the others, so most of these are in `internal/kinds/http`;
`internal/proxy` keeps only what belongs to the engine itself — the
kind registry, socket activation and the address matching a reload
uses.

Six tests exist for the split itself:

| Test | Covers |
|------|--------|
| `cmd/*/main_test.go: TestLinkedKinds` | Each binary links exactly its own role's kinds and no others. A stray blank import is how that quietly stops being true |
| `internal/proxy: TestUnlinkedKindRefused` | Every kind in the roster, configured validly, is refused by a binary that did not link it — with the daemon that does serve it named. Before the split an unknown kind fell through to the HTTP data plane |
| `internal/daemon: TestOwnTakesOnlyItsShare` | The three roles take disjoint parts of one estate configuration and between them take all of it, and `own` does not modify the configuration it was given |
| `internal/kinds/http: TestReloadThatCannotBindKeepsTheOldRoutes` | The data plane compiles its generation before the listeners are bound, so a bind that fails after that has to put it back: the refused generation's routes are never served and its upstream never takes a request, the live generation counter does not move, and the failure is counted |
| `internal/kinds/http: TestRequestOnTheOldGenerationSurvivesAReload` | A request admitted before a reload finishes on the generation it started with, on the upstream it started with, while the reload itself returns without waiting for it — the two halves of the `Prepare`/`commit`/`Retire` handover |
| `internal/daemon: TestRunStartsReloadsAndStops` | A whole daemon, driven the way a service manager drives one: ready, answering the management socket, reopening its logs and reloading on a signal, rolling back to the generation recorded at start, and leaving on SIGTERM with the socket gone. The body of `xproxy`, `xgate` and `xrelay` is one function, and what only runs on a signal or through the socket is not reached by the parts tested separately |

| Test | Covers |
|------|--------|
| `TestProxyBasics` | Routing, strip prefix, header operations, forwarding headers with trusted and untrusted peers, traversal, no route, method filter, round robin distribution, dead upstream 502, rate limit 429 with `Retry-After`, tarpit delay, ACL 403, WebSocket refusal, redirect and respond actions, body limit 413, sticky cookie attributes and stickiness, URI length 414, counters and upstream statistics |
| `TestRetryOnDeadEndpoint` | Retry to a second endpoint, outlier ejection of the dead one, no replay of POST |
| `TestReload` | Generation swap changes routing, listener change refused, reload counters |
| `TestTLSAndRedirect` | HTTP to HTTPS 308 preserving path and query, TLS 1.3 with HTTP/2 negotiated, `X-Forwarded-Proto`, TLS 1.2 refused when the minimum is 1.3 |
| `TestLocalClusterSharesBans` | Two nodes over Unix sockets, no certificate: a ban placed on one reaches the other, the peer is reported under the uid the kernel gave rather than the node id it announced, and an unlisted uid is refused and its bans are not applied |
| `TestTCPPassthrough` | Two HTTPS origins with their own certificates behind a `kind: tcp` listener: routed by server name end to end (the client verifies the origin's certificate; a wildcard route to the wrong origin fails the client's check, proving no termination), non-TLS bytes to the default echo upstream, unknown name closed on a listener without default, counters and pool accounting, idle timeout |
| `TestQUICPassthrough` | A quic-go echo server behind a `kind: tcp` listener with `quic: true`: streams echo end to end with the origin's certificate verified by the client, flow counters, an unknown name never reaches an endpoint and is counted, the flow ends after the idle timeout with bytes accounted |
| `TestTCPProxyProtocol` | The upstream receives a PROXY v2 header (signature, command, family, client port) followed by the client's bytes |
| `TestForwardProxy` | A `kind: forward` listener as the proxy of an `http.Client`: CONNECT tunnel to an HTTPS origin verified end to end, plain relay with hop-by-hop headers removed and `Via` both ways, refusals for an unlisted port, a denied name, a denied CIDR, an unresolvable name, an origin-form request and (on the default policy) a private address; 407 with `Proxy-Authenticate`, wrong password refused, the right one tunnels, a destination outside the allow list refused with credentials, a rotated users file takes effect on reload and a broken one fails the reload; the tunnel bound answers 503, bytes sent before the 200 reach the origin, idle tunnels close; counters and no tunnel left after shutdown |
| `TestHoneypot` | Built-in decoy with a spoofed `Server` header, HEAD without body, a body file with a delay, the third hit trips a `honeypot` ban trigger, marks listed with hits and route, unmark, a vanished body file fails the reload |
| `TestEveryDecoyIsPlausibleAndHarmless`, `TestDecoyListsAgree`, `TestEveryDecoyServesThroughARoute`, `TestHoneypotExampleIsSound`; `internal/config` `TestDecoyReferenceComplete`, `TestRFCReferenceIsWellFormed` | The whole decoy table judged as one, so a decoy added later cannot skip the rules: each body is long enough to be believed and short enough to be an inline body, its content type parses and carries no line break, a body claiming JSON or XML parses as one and a body claiming HTML has a document element, and each ends with a newline. A body that assigns a password, a token or a key must carry a marker a reader recognises as fake, and a private key block must decode to a message saying it is a decoy — with a count of how many bodies each rule judged, so a pattern that stops matching cannot turn the rule into a no-op. The names the proxy serves, the names the validator accepts and the rows in the configuration reference are three lists in three places and must agree exactly. Every decoy is then served through a real route: its own body and content type, nothing reaching the upstream, one mark for the client however many it touched, and HEAD without a body. The shipped example is parsed and checked for a decoy it never demonstrates, a path two routes both claim, a path that is not a path, and a catch-all that is not last; docs/RFC.md's own shape holds, with a defined status on every row, a reason on every refusal and a heading behind every entry in its contents |

The probe is asserted to run *at once*: the health interval is an hour, so an endpoint that took the ordinary start jitter would still be unhealthy when the test ends -- without that assertion "waits for a probe" would silently mean "is out of the pool for up to an interval". The exceptions are driven through the state that produces them rather than asserted from the outside: the other endpoints are made unhealthy, then draining, and the draining case matters because draining is an operator's decision and a pool being taken out of service is exactly when the new endpoint is needed. The two non-exceptions are pinned in the other direction -- the endpoints a pool starts with, discovery's own first resolution included, must not wait, or every restart would serve nothing for an interval; and an address that leaves and comes back must wait again, because the process listening there now is not the one that was healthy before |

A nine-address answer against a bound of three installs three, counts one truncation and is **not** counted as a failed resolution -- the pool is serving, on fewer endpoints than were announced. The answer is given in an order that is not the sorted one, so the assertion that the first three by address are the ones kept cannot pass by accident; the same nine addresses announced rotated must produce the same subset and no change to the pool, because a subset that moved would remove and add endpoints every interval. The boundary is pinned in both directions (exactly at the bound is not over it), and an unset bound falls back to the default rather than to no bound, which is the case a configuration built in code rather than loaded reaches. The config test covers the default being filled in and both ends of the range |

The resolver is driven through the rule that matters most -- **a value is never in an error** -- by asserting that a wrong-key failure names the reference and that no line of either key's PEM appears in the message; the mutation that interpolates the material fails it. The external signer is tested against a stand-in helper: a real TLS handshake completes with the private key in another process, a helper holding a different key fails at *build* rather than at the first handshake, and every way a helper can answer badly -- a wrong signature, a megabyte of nothing, silence -- is an error. Validation is driven through the combinations rather than the happy path: two custody keys set, none set, a vault reference with no vault, a vault reference with no field, `http://` with neither opt-in and with only one, and `insecure` on an `https://` address, which is refused outright because skipping verification there means anything on the path can hand this proxy the keys it will serve with. The sandbox test asserts the signer's socket is a **write** rule and not a read one -- in a directory of its own, because the management socket would otherwise have made the read/write mutation pass -- and that a `vault:` reference becomes no path rule at all. The FIPS probe is a measurement, so its test is too: `required: true` without the module refuses to start and the message names `GODEBUG=fips140=on`. Every guard is mutation-checked | A private key is what every other control here exists to protect, and the three ways to get it are a file somebody can read, a log line somebody can grep, and a sandbox rule that silently was not derived |
| `internal/signerd`, `cmd/xsigner` | The signing helper, driven against the proxy's own client rather than a stand-in: `TestAHandshakeWithTheKeyInAnotherProcess` completes a real HTTPS request for each key type with the private key in another goroutine's process-shaped world, over TLS 1.3 *and* 1.2, because the RSA path differs between them -- 1.3 uses PSS and needs the salt length on the wire, 1.2 uses PKCS#1 v1.5. Ed25519 has its own case for the reason it is the one most likely to be written wrongly: it signs a message rather than a pre-hash, and the mutation that pre-hashes it fails only that subtest. Then every refusal: a key it does not hold, a version from the future, a digest that is not base64, a hash nobody names, and the three shapes of *right request, wrong key* -- PSS asked of an ECDSA key and so on -- which matter because signing them anyway produces a signature the verifier rejects three layers from its cause. A refusal for an unknown key is asserted **not** to list the keys the helper holds, since a caller that can enumerate them learns which certificates the machine serves. `TestADocumentIsNotADigest` is the bound that keeps the socket from being a general-purpose signing oracle, and `TestOneConnectionAnswersManyRequests` pins the pooling the proxy depends on. The command's tests run the real entry point: `-validate` loads every key before the socket is bound, `-print-keys` prints the public half that loaded and is checked to carry no private material, a world-writable socket mode is refused, a vault over plain HTTP is refused (there is no insecure escape hatch here, unlike the proxy's own client), and the helper is started, signs for its own key through `keysource.CertificateFor`, and stops on a signal. `TestAFloodOfRefusalsIsOneLogLine` drives two hundred refusals through one connection and requires at most three log lines and exactly two hundred counted, because the bound belongs on the log and never on the counters -- a counter that stopped counting under a flood would hide the flood it exists to show. Every guard is mutation-checked | A helper is a signing oracle for the keys it holds. The ways that goes wrong are: signing something that is not a digest, signing with a key the request did not mean, telling a caller which keys exist, and letting anything on the machine connect |

Every guard is mutation-checked. Over the socket the whole round trip is driven: ask, two refused approvals, one that works, a second from the same person refused, the active grant in the listing, and both acts with their note in the file on disk; the statuses are asserted separately (404, 403, 409, 400) because a client has to tell a refusal it can act on from one it cannot. | `internal/kinds/forward` `TestServeErrorIgnoresBothWaysAListenerEnds`, `TestForwardIsNotAFlowCounter`; `internal/proxy` `TestNewReleasesThePlaneWhenALaterStepFails` | The three regressions the split introduced, each pinned where the move lost something. The forward instance must not satisfy `proxy.FlowCounter`, because the engine folds that into `quic_flows_open` and the tunnels it counts are already in `forward_tunnels_open`. | `internal/mqtt`, `internal/kinds/mqtt` (topic bounds, Sparkplug B) | `TestSparkplugTopicsAreReadByTheirShape`, `TestTheSparkplugSequenceIsReadWithoutTheMetrics`; `TestMQTTPerTopicBounds`, `TestMQTTSparkplugPolicy`, `TestARuleRefusesRetainTheListenerWouldAllow`, `TestOneSequenceGapIsOneRefusal`, `TestTheEdgeNodeTableIsBoundedAndForwardsRatherThanRefuses`, `TestADeathEndsTheNode` | The topic reader first, because the whole policy rests on what a topic says a message is: the five shapes the convention defines pass, a device type without a device and a node type with one are both refused (which is what stops a DDATA missing its device being read as something else), and a topic that is nearly a Sparkplug topic -- an unknown message type, an empty level, too few or too many levels -- is not one. The payload reader is tested for what it must *not* do: it reads the sequence past a metric set it skips by length, including a long one, and it stops rather than reading past the end of a payload cut short in each wire type, a key that is not a varint, or a group it cannot skip -- a payload half read is a sequence half known. A payload with no sequence reports none, so the check is skipped rather than the message refused. Then both policies end to end through a real relay: a payload inside the topic's own bound passes while one inside the *listener's* bound is refused, a QoS the topic refuses and one it requires are both caught (the second silently at QoS 0, where there is no acknowledgement to refuse with, which is why the counter is the assertion), and retain is refused on the topic that does not allow it while the configuration topic that does keeps it. For Sparkplug: data before a birth is refused, the birth then data with the next sequence pass, a sequence that skips is refused, a command from a publisher outside `command_clients` is refused although its message type is allowed, a type the list does not name is refused, and on a listener declared to carry nothing but Sparkplug a topic that is not one is refused. Four properties an end-to-end test cannot see close the rest: a rule refusing retain on a listener that allows it (the narrower of the two winning, which is the opposite direction from the listener refusal above), a sequence gap refusing *once* and the next message in step passing -- because a state that did not move would refuse the whole fleet for ever after one lost packet -- through the wrap at 255, a fifth edge node past `max_nodes` being forwarded rather than refused while the nodes the table does hold are still checked (a bound that refuses is an outage caused by a bound), and a death clearing the birth and the sequence so that data before the next birth is out of order |
| `internal/iec104`, `internal/kinds/iec104` (IEC 60870-5-104) | `TestTheThreeFormatsAreReadFromTheControlOctets`, `TestFramesThatAreNotFrames`, `TestTheASDUHeaderIsReadFieldByField`, `TestAddressesAreReadInBothShapes`, `TestTheSelectBitIsReadFromTheCommandQualifier`, `TestTheReaderReadsFrameAfterFrame`, `TestTheNamesAreTheStandardsOwn`, `TestTypesAreClassifiedByWhatTheyDo`; `TestARuleNarrowsByEveryFieldItNames`, `TestTheCommonAddressListIsABoundary`, `TestARuleCanNameOneHalfOfATwoStepCommand`, `TestNamingAControlActivationNamesItsConfirmation`, `TestASelectionDiesWithItsConnection`, `TestARuleCanBeLimitedToAWindow`, `TestAnObserveRuleDecidesNothing`; `TestIEC104ReverseRelay`, `TestACommandOutsideThePolicyNeverReachesTheStation`, `TestOnlyAnActivationIsNegativelyConfirmed`, `TestMonitorOnlyCannotBeRuledThrough`, `TestSelectBeforeOperateIsEnforced`, `TestADeactivationWithdrawsASelection`, `TestTheControlFunctionsAreAPolicyToo`, `TestASequenceGapIsFoundAndIsOneRefusal`, `TestAStationMayNotCommandItsControlCentre`, `TestMalformedFramesEndTheSession`, `TestShadowModeRecordsWhatItWouldHaveRefused` | The wire format first, because a relay that cannot read a frame cannot decide about it and the substation behind it will read those octets somehow. The three formats are read from the control octets rather than inferred from the length, the sequence numbers are asserted at the top of the 15-bit space (a relay that truncated there would start refusing a station's frames once a link had been up long enough), and the ASDU header is read field by field -- including the two flags that *share* the cause octet, because a relay reading the whole octet as the cause would see COT 199 and refuse a station's own negative confirmation. What must not parse: no start octet, a length that lies, a length below the four control octets and above what the octet can describe, an ASDU too short for a header, an object count that does not agree with the octets, and an S or U frame whose length says it carries a body -- that last one because it is the shape an ASDU is smuggled past a check that only looks at I frames. Addresses are read in both shapes the standard allows, and on a sequence only the one address that is *on the wire* is reported: reporting the implied ones would be inventing them. A type identification the standard does not define is forwarded with its addresses unread rather than guessed at, since inventing an object size would misreport an address and a policy would then decide about the wrong point. The select bit is read in both shapes, and a measurement must not appear to carry a selection. Then the policy as a unit: every matcher a rule is made of is narrowed one field at a time and each must fall through -- the type list and the client list among them, because a type list that matched everything would let a reset process command through a rule written for a breaker. The common-address list is checked before the rules *and* before `default_action: allow`, the select and execute halves are shown to be a four-eyes control (one client may arm and not fire, the other the reverse, and a measurement is neither), and a control-function list is shown to name pairs, since naming an activation without its confirmation would leave the control centre waiting for ever. The selection table is tested for the four things that make it a safety property rather than a cache: a selection cannot be taken by another connection, dies when its own connection closes, expires, and authorises one execution. Then the relay end to end against a fake substation gateway that answers the U-format handshake and numbers its own frames: the ordinary link with telemetry up and an allowed command down; a command outside the policy refused with the standard's own negative confirmation and never reaching the station, with the link surviving it; a *measurement* refused with no answer at all, because there is nothing in the protocol to negatively confirm about one; `monitor_only` that a rule allowing every class cannot override, with telemetry still flowing; select-before-operate with a bare execute refused, the two steps in order passing, a selection not reusable and not transferable to another point; a deactivation withdrawing one; `STOPDT_act` refused while `TESTFR_act` and its confirmation pass; a replayed sequence number found and the link recovering on the next frame in step; a station sending an activation to its own control centre refused; four malformed frames each ending the session; and shadow mode carrying the command it would have refused while the ledger keeps the sample -- and still refusing the malformed frame, because integrity is not policy |

`TestTheVersionListDecidesBeforeAnythingElse`, `TestTheCredentialIsReadPerVersionAndDoesNotCrossOver`, `TestTheSecurityLevelIsAFloorNotADecoration`, `TestReadOnlyCannotBeOverriddenByARule`, `TestTheDirectionOfTheMessageHasToMatchTheListener`, `TestASubtreeIsComparedPerSubIdentifierNotPerCharacter`, `TestADenyListInsideAnAllowedSubtreeIsAnException`, `TestAWriteListReplacesTheReadListForASetRequest`, `TestARuleWithARepetitionBoundDoesNotCoverTrafficPastIt`, `TestAnObserveRuleDoesNotDecide`, `TestAScheduleLimitsARuleToItsWindow`, `TestARuleCanNameTheContext`, `TestEveryBadRuleIsRefusedAtLoad`; `TestTheTableRefusesRatherThanForgets`, `TestTheStreamReaderDecidesAboutALengthBeforeReadingIt`, `TestWhatCanAndCannotBeRewritten`, `TestTheRepetitionCountIsLoweredOrTheRequestIsRefused`, `TestTheAnswerIsRestoredToTheVersionTheQuestionUsed`; `TestSNMPRelaysThePollItWasConfiguredFor`, `TestASetRequestIsRefusedWithTheErrorAManagerDisplays`, `TestAGetBulkIsLoweredRatherThanRefused`, `TestAnAuthenticatedWalkPastTheBoundIsRefusedNotRewritten`, `TestAnAnswerDisproportionateToItsQuestionIsRefused`, `TestAnAnswerPastTheSizeBoundIsRefused`, `TestAResponseNobodyAskedForIsNotForwarded`, `TestAMalformedDatagramIsRefusedWhateverThePolicySays`, `TestShadowModeRecordsTheRefusalAndForwardsTheMessage`, `TestAClientOutsideTheListIsRefusedEvenInShadowMode`, `TestAVersionThreeTrapIsDowngradedForALegacyCollector`, `TestAVersionThreeRequestIsNotDowngraded`, `TestTheAnswerComesBackInTheVersionTheManagerSpoke`, `TestTheRateLimitHoldsInShadowMode`, `TestTheStreamTransportCarriesTheSamePolicy`, `TestAStreamWithTheWrongFramingEnds`, `TestAStreamMessagePastTheBoundIsRefusedUnread`, `TestADatagramPastTheBoundIsRefused`, `TestTheStreamSideTakesTLSAndTheAgentSideDoesNot`, `TestAStreamAnswerNobodyAskedForEndsTheSession`, `TestARequestFromTheAgentSideIsRefused`, `TestANotificationFromTheAgentSideOfADatagramRelayIsRefused`, `TestTheConnectionBoundHolds` | The wire format first, and the encoder beside it, because this is the one relay kind that *writes* messages as well as reading them. BER is read with the application tags SNMP adds (Counter32, Gauge32, TimeTicks, Opaque, IpAddress, Counter64) and the eight context-constructed PDU tags, and the version field is asserted off-by-one from its name because a v1 message carries 0. Two encoding details have their own tests because getting either wrong is a silent policy hole: an object identifier's *packed* first sub-identifier is itself base-128, so 2.999 is two octets and a reader that took one would read a different object under a different subtree; and an INTEGER is written in the fewest octets that keep its sign, so a rewritten field is shaped like one nobody touched. The rewrites are asserted to carry the bindings through unchanged, to the octet: what was asked for is not the relay's to edit, only how much of it may come back and in whose name it is asked. Then the policy as a unit, narrowed one matcher at a time, with two properties that are the point of the kind. The credential sets do not cross over -- a rule naming community strings cannot match a v3 message and one naming users cannot match a v2c one, because letting either would apply a rule written about one authentication scheme to another -- and `read_only` is checked above the rules and shown to survive a rule that allows every write, because a read-only listener one rule could write through is not one. The subtree comparison is asserted on the pair that separates a structural test from a string one: `1.3.6.1.2.1` covers `1.3.6.1.2.1.1.1.0` and does not cover `1.3.6.1.2.11`. A refusal is asserted *not* to carry the community string, because a security log of guessed credentials is a list of the estate's passwords. Then the relay end to end against a fake agent on both transports, recording every message that reached it -- which is the assertion that matters on a security relay: not what the manager was told, but what got through to the equipment. A write through a read-only relay never arrives and the manager gets the Response PDU its version has a word for, because a dropped request is a timeout and a timeout is what a dead device looks like. A GETBULK of ten thousand repetitions arrives at the agent as the bound, with its request identifier and bindings intact, and the manager still gets an answer -- the trade that makes the bound deployable. An authenticated v3 walk past the bound is refused instead and no forged answer is sent, because there is no key here to re-authenticate a rewritten message with. Both response bounds are exercised from the agent's side, where there is nobody to send an error to, and an answer whose request identifier nobody used is dropped rather than forwarded: on a datagram transport that is the shape of a spoofing attack on the manager. The version rewrite is tested in the three shapes that matter: a v3 trap reaching a legacy collector as v2c with a community string the device never knew, a v3 request refused rather than half-translated, and a v1/v2c pair where the question goes out in one version and the answer comes back in the other. On the stream side, RFC 6353 TLS towards the manager with plain v2c towards the agent, an HTTP request ending the session rather than being resynchronised past, and a declared length of sixty thousand octets refused with none of them sent. And shadow mode carries the write it would have refused while the ledger keeps the sample -- and still refuses the malformed datagram, the rate limit and the client list, because a relay whose amplification bounds were in shadow mode would be a working amplifier |

`TestABindWithAnEmptyPasswordIsRefusedByDefault`, `TestAPasswordIsNotCarriedInTheClear`, `TestTheVersionIsAFloor`, `TestTheSASLMechanismListDecides`, `TestReadOnlyCannotBeOverriddenByARule`, `TestTheBaseSuffixesAreABoundary`, `TestTheAttributePolicyAppliesToTheQuestionAndTheAnswer`, `TestTheFilterBoundsHold`, `TestARuleCanNameTheIdentity`, `TestARuleNarrowsByScopeSubtreeAndWindow`, `TestAnExtendedOperationIsNamedByItsOID`, `TestControlsAndTheOperationsWithNoDecision`, `TestObserveAndTheDefault`, `TestTheEntryBoundIsCarriedWithTheDecision`, `TestACompareIsAReadAndIsCoveredByTheAttributePolicy`, `TestEveryBadRuleIsRefusedAtLoad`; `TestLDAPRelaysTheSearchItWasConfiguredFor`, `TestABindWithAnEmptyPasswordNeverReachesTheDirectory`, `TestAPasswordInTheClearIsRefusedEvenInShadowMode`, `TestAPasswordHashIsRemovedFromAnAnswerThatCarriesIt`, `TestAskingForAPasswordByNameIsRefused`, `TestASearchIsCutAtTheEntryBound`, `TestTheIdentityIsAdoptedOnlyWhenTheDirectoryAgrees`, `TestStartTLSIsTerminatedHereAndDiscardsTheIdentity`, `TestStartTLSOnAListenerThatHasNoneIsAnsweredNotForwarded`, `TestAMalformedMessageEndsTheSession`, `TestMessageIdentifierZeroIsRefused`, `TestAnEntryForASearchNobodyMadeIsDropped`, `TestTheBindRateIsBoundedSeparately`, `TestShadowModeRecordsTheRefusalAndForwardsTheRequest`, `TestAClientOutsideTheListIsRefusedEvenInShadowMode`, `TestAMessagePastTheBoundIsRefusedUnread`, `TestAStreamWithTheWrongFramingEnds`, `TestASearchOutsideTheNamingContextNeverReachesTheDirectory` | The wire format first, and the bind before anything else, because on this protocol the bind *is* the finding: a simple bind's two string fields make four different security statements and the reader has to tell them apart. A name with an empty password is an unauthenticated bind, which RFC 4513 calls anonymous and most directories answer with success -- so the test asserts all four readings, including the one nobody notices. Filters are read as a shape and asserted as one: the term count, the nesting depth, the attributes named, and whether a substring term begins with a wildcard no index can serve, with an anchored substring shown *not* to count as one. What must not parse: not a sequence, one field, an operation nobody defined, a bind with two fields or an authentication choice nobody defined, a SASL bind with no mechanism, a search with seven fields or a scope nobody defined, an empty and/or (which the grammar makes true and false and which a directory would then evaluate as a filter nobody wrote), a presence item with no attribute, a control with no name, and two messages concatenated -- that last one because on a stream it is a framing the reader did not agree to and forwarding it forwards two messages as one. The delete operation gets its own case because its whole body *is* the distinguished name rather than the first field of a sequence, and a reader that expected a sequence would refuse every delete. Distinguished names are the other half of the wire tests: the escaping, the quoted form, the hex escapes, and a suffix compared one relative name at a time -- with dc=notexample,dc=com asserted against a suffix of dc=example,dc=com, because a string suffix test admits it. Then the policy as a unit, narrowed one matcher at a time, with the three things it refuses above every rule: a password in the clear (marked hard, so shadow mode does not carry it), read_only against a rule that allows every write, and the naming contexts. The attribute policy is tested in all three of its shapes, because they are three different statements: named in the request (refused, because stripping would answer a plain question with a silence the client cannot distinguish from an empty directory), named in the filter (refused, because (userPassword=a*) is a password oracle a character at a time), and asked for with * (allowed, and removed from the answer -- the half a request-side access list cannot do). Then the relay end to end against a fake directory that records every message that reached it, which is the assertion that matters: not what the client was told, but what got through. A bind with an empty password never arrives and the client is told invalidCredentials rather than success; a password hash the directory *did* send is removed from the entry while the entry, its name and the search's completion survive; a search of twenty entries is cut at three with the directory's own sizeLimitExceeded and the directory's own Done is dropped, because two answers to one request is worse than a truncated one; the identity is shown to be the directory's to grant, by binding with the wrong password and finding the rule keyed on that identity refuses; StartTLS is answered here rather than forwarded, and discards the identity, and a listener with no TLS to offer says so in the protocol's own terms; and shadow mode carries the modify it would have refused while the ledger keeps the sample -- and still refuses the malformed message, the oversize one, message identifier zero, the client list and the cleartext password, because none of those is policy |

`TestTheOrdinaryLeaseCycleGoesThrough`, `TestAReplyFromAnAddressThatIsNotAServerIsDropped`, `TestAnOfferArrivingOnTheSegmentIsNotRelayed`, `TestTheDangerousOptionsAreStrippedByDefault`, `TestAGatewayTheEstateDoesNotHaveIsRemoved`, `TestAnAllowedRouteGoesThroughAndAnotherDoesNot`, `TestAClientsOwnAgentOptionIsStripped`, `TestAnOptionAClientMayNotAskForIsRemovedFromTheAsk`, `TestALeasePastTheBoundIsShortenedRatherThanRefused`, `TestAHiddenOptionIsRefusedEvenInShadowMode`, `TestARuleNarrowsByHardwareAddressAndVendorClass`, `TestABootFileOutsideTheListIsRefused`, `TestTheStarvationBoundIsKeyedOnTheHardwareAddress`, `TestAReplyNobodyAskedForIsDropped`, `TestTooManyHopsIsRefused`, `TestAClientIdentifierThatNamesAnotherAddressIsRefused`, `TestADenyRuleRefusesAndNakAnswersWhenAsked`, `TestDropIsTheDefaultRefusal`, `TestAReplyIsBroadcastOnlyWhenThereIsNowhereToUnicast`, `TestASilentServerLeavesNothingBehind` | The wire format first, and the two encodings that make one message say different things to different parsers before anything else, because those are the finding. Option 52 says the sname and file fields carry options, so the test hides a WPAD URL in the boot filename field and asserts it is *read* -- a reader that missed it would be deciding about a message the server reads differently -- and RFC 3396 says an option appearing twice is one option whose value is the concatenation, so the test splits a boot filename across two instances and asserts the joined value, the recorded Split flag and the first-appearance ordering a relay has to write back. hlen is asserted against a field left full of rubbish, because Infiniband addresses are twenty octets and the padding after a six-octet one is whatever the client's stack left there. What must not parse: the wrong magic cookie (the options are BOOTP's, not DHCP's), an op nobody defined, an hlen of 200, an option with no length octet, an option claiming more than the field has left (built without the padding a real client sends, because padding *is* part of the options field and an option running into it is readable), and a message with no option 53 -- that last one because a message with no type is BOOTP and this relay does not speak for BOOTP. The fuzzer found the one the table did not: option 53 present, one octet, and **zero**, which is the absent value wearing a length and which no standard defines, so the corpus entry stays as the regression. The encoder is held to the reader: a removed option is gone rather than annotated, a 600-octet option is split the way the reader joins one, a zero-length option survives (several are present-or-absent flags), and the overload encoding is read and deliberately not written, because a relay whose own output hid options in the boot filename field would be making its output harder to read than its input. The turn that makes the kind usable gets its own test: naming what an option may *contain* on a rule is what allows the option, so the build segment's replies keep options 66 and 67 while every other segment's have them stripped -- and a rule's own deny still beats its own permission. Then the relay end to end against a fake DHCP server that answers from a socket of its own and records every message that reached it. The rogue check is tested by pointing allow_servers at a network the real server is not on, so the *legitimate* reply is refused, which is the check itself rather than a proxy for it; an offer injected on the client port is counted and not relayed; a reply carrying a route, a WPAD URL and a boot file arrives at the client with none of them and with its address, mask and lease intact; a reply naming a gateway the estate does not have is stripped *and reported as a finding*, because the source was an admitted server and that is what a compromised one looks like; a client's own option 82 is replaced by the relay's; an option a client may not ask for is trimmed from its parameter list rather than the message refused; shadow mode carries the message type it would have refused and still refuses the split option; the starvation limit is shown to be keyed on the hardware address by having a second address sail past the first one's exhausted bucket; a reply whose transaction identifier matches no request is dropped; and the pending table is shown to refuse a third request rather than forget one of two, and then to empty itself on the timeout with no traffic at all -- which is the defect the test found, because the gauge was only republished when a message arrived and a quiet segment is exactly when somebody looks at it |

`TestAReadTransferGoesThroughAndTheServerSeesTheRequest`, `TestATraversalNeverReachesTheServer`, `TestAWriteIsRefusedByDefault`, `TestTheWindowIsLoweredRatherThanRefused`, `TestADeclaredTransferSizePastTheBoundIsRefusedBeforeAnOctetMoves`, `TestATransferIsCutAtItsByteBound`, `TestAServerThatAcceptsMoreThanItWasOfferedEndsTheTransfer`, `TestADataPacketLargerThanTheNegotiatedBlockEndsTheTransfer`, `TestADatagramFromAThirdAddressIsDropped`, `TestAPacketThatIsNotARequestOnThePortIsRefused`, `TestDropAnswersNothing`, `TestShadowModeForwardsPolicyAndStillRefusesTheHardShapes`, `TestAServerErrorReachesTheClient`, `TestASilentServerEndsTheTransferOnTheIdleTimeout`, `TestTheTransferBoundRefusesTheSecondClient`, `TestARuleNarrowsByDirectionDirectoryAndWindow`, `TestAClientOutsideTheListNeverReachesTheServer` | The wire format first, and the *fields* before anything else, because on this protocol the filename is the finding. A field with no terminating zero is refused rather than read to the end of the packet: "to the end of the packet" is one server's reading and another parser may take a different one, and a filename that runs past its field is exactly where the two differ. An acknowledgement's body must be exactly two octets for the same reason, and an option whose value is not a number is refused rather than ignored, because a relay that ignored one would be bounding a number the transfer is not using. Then the classifier, as a table of the spellings a deny list would have had to think of separately -- `../../etc/shadow`, `firmware/../firmware/x`, `..\..\etc\shadow` (asserted as a *traversal* and not merely a backslash, because an estate serving Windows hosts may allow backslashes), `/etc/shadow`, `\\host\share\x`, `c:\x`, `C:x`, `secret.txt.`, `secret.txt ` -- each collapsing to one of a handful of shapes, with the three that no configuration may allow pinned as such and a separate test that the refusal's *detail* never repeats the name or carries a control octet, because a name with an escape sequence in it is the reason for the refusal and repeating it raw is the same mistake one layer up. The encoder is held to what the parser holds a peer to: a NUL in a filename, a mode, an option name or an option value is refused rather than written, since the packet would then say something other than what the relay decided. Two fuzz targets: the reader (every datagram arrives before anything has authenticated anything, because there is nothing to authenticate with) and the classifier (it must answer for every filename, and a plain path must never clean to something with `..` in it that the name did not have). Then the policy as a unit, narrowed one matcher at a time, with the shadow-mode line drawn twice: a bound and a hard path class are marked so that shadow mode still refuses them, and a direction or a directory is not. Then the relay end to end against a fake TFTP server that answers from a socket of its own -- as RFC 1350 requires, which is what exercises the half of this kind that has to follow a transfer onto an ephemeral port pair -- and that records every request that reached it, which is the assertion that matters: not what the client was told, but what got through. An observation naming nothing identifiable is counted and refused rather than making a record with no identity in it; a hardware address and a bare address become one record the moment one observation carries both; and the case that must **not** merge gets its own test -- a hardware address nobody has seen, at an address another record holds *with a hardware address of its own*, which is two machines sharing one address over time. That test was written first and forced the design: merging them would overwrite the older machine's vendor, role and history with the newer machine's, which is exactly the history an incident needs, so the finding is recorded on the record that *lost* the address -- because the new device gets a record of its own and the old one would otherwise simply stop appearing, and "stopped appearing" is not a finding anybody reads. Then the classifier, held to the claim the package makes about itself: behaviour outranks self-description, so a device whose vendor class says PLC and whose behaviour says workstation is classified by the behaviour; the control-protocol roles come from the *direction* of the exchange, because on Modbus the controller answers and the HMI asks; writing is what separates an HMI from a historian; a vendor prefix concludes a role only where the manufacturer makes one kind of device; and two rules of equal weight that disagree produce an ambiguous verdict with ten points off rather than a silent winner. What the rules must not do is guess: an asset no rule matches is `unknown`, not the nearest thing. The bound is asserted to *evict* rather than refuse, which is the opposite of every other bounded table in the proxy and is the point -- forgetting here loses history rather than making a decision wrong. The store round-trips the inventory *and the baseline*, since an inventory that came back without its baseline would report the whole estate as new. On the engine side: observing without an inventory is a no-op that counts nothing, so a kind carries no conditional; the summary and the Prometheus families are *absent* rather than zero when there is no inventory, so a dashboard does not show an estate of nothing to somebody who never asked for one; an unexpected role is counted on every observation rather than once at first sighting; a state file that will not read is a warning and the proxy still starts, while a malformed vendor file refuses to start, because one is a record and the other is configuration an operator trusted. The control plane is tested for the two things a filter must not do quietly -- a role that is not a role is refused rather than matching nothing, since an empty list reads as "the estate is clean", and a list cut by `top` reports how many matched -- and freezing a baseline is shown to make the *next* device new and forgetting it to make nothing new, which found the defect: `Thaw` had left the flag set, so the summary said "no baseline" while the devices said "new" |

`TestTheOrdinaryConnectionGoesThroughAndTheServerSeesIt`, `TestARefusedStatementNeverReachesTheServer`, `TestTheSecondStatementAfterASemicolonIsDecidedToo`, `TestCopyFromProgramNeverReachesTheServer`, `TestAnUnreadableStatementIsRefusedAndNamed`, `TestAStatementSentAsAParseIsDecided`, `TestAClientThatWillNotEncryptIsRefused`, `TestAReplicationConnectionIsRefusedAtTheStartupPacket`, `TestAWeakAuthenticationMethodEndsTheConnection`, `TestAStatementBeforeAuthenticationIsRefused`, `TestShadowModeForwardsPolicyAndStillRefusesTheHardShapes`, `TestDropClosesWithoutAnAnswer`, `TestAClientOutsideTheListNeverReachesTheServer`, `TestAServerThatRefusesTLSIsRefusedWhenTheModeRequiresIt`, `TestARuleWidensTheListenerForItsOwnUser` | The wire format's three irregularities first, because each is where a relay either does its job or quietly fails to. The first message has no type octet, so the four shapes it can be -- a version 3 startup, an SSL request, a GSSAPI encryption request, a cancel -- are each asserted read by their *code*, and a reader that confused them is caught by a fuzz invariant rather than a table: a packet that parsed as a cancel must not also carry parameters. What must not parse is a length of zero or one (self-referential, so subtracting four gives a huge unsigned number, which is the classic allocation bug), a length that disagrees with what is there, an SSL request with a body, protocol version 2 (removed from the server in 14, and a different packet shape, so a relay that forwarded one would be forwarding octets it had not read), and trailing rubbish after the parameter terminator -- refused rather than trimmed, because a server reads the message the sender framed. The length's off-by-four gets its own test, since getting it wrong by one loses the framing of every message after it, and Sync's empty body is asserted too because a reader that required a body would refuse it. Five octets mean different things in each direction and are asserted both ways, because a relay with one table would read a row of somebody's data as a request to describe a portal. Then the classifier, as the table of spellings a deny list would have had to think of separately -- `DR/**/OP`, `SEL/**/ECT`, `SEL--x\nECT`, `/* /* nested */ */ SELECT`, `SELECT 'DROP TABLE t'`, `SELECT $tag$ DROP $tag$`, `SELECT "DROP"` -- each either classified correctly or, crucially, landing on `unknown`, which is refused. A comment is asserted to be *whitespace rather than nothing*, so a keyword split on purpose does not reassemble. The three conservative cases are pinned as such: a data-modifying CTE is the write it contains, `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` is the statement it runs while plain `EXPLAIN` is not, and `COPY` carries which of its three operations it is. Text that cannot be lexed is refused rather than classified, and that is tested for each of the four unterminated things. Then the policy as a unit, with the shadow-mode line drawn on seven refusals and the two that no configuration can lift asserted by compiling a configuration that tries: `unknown` is not a nameable kind and `program` is not a nameable COPY target, on the listener and on a rule both. Then the relay end to end against a fake PostgreSQL server that completes a startup and records every statement that reached it, which is the assertion that matters -- not what the client was told. A refused statement is shown never to arrive *and* the connection shown still usable afterwards, because a relay that ended the session would make an application retry the whole transaction; `SELECT 1; DROP TABLE users` is shown to be refused whole; a DELETE sent as a Parse rather than a Query is refused, which is the half of the protocol every modern driver actually uses; a statement sent before the server said AuthenticationOk is shown never to reach it; and a server that answers `N` to the relay's own SSL request is refused when the mode requires TLS, with the startup packet asserted never to have crossed the unencrypted leg. The require_tls test asserts the *load* fails without a certificate, because a listener that silently served plaintext instead is the bug. One bug the tests found: ANALYZE is itself a maintenance statement, so the word that told us EXPLAIN executes was being read as the statement it executes |

`TestTheDefaultsAreTheOnesThatMatter`, `TestTheReplicationCommandsAreRefusedHard`, `TestSetOptionCannotLiftTheStrippedCapability`, `TestChangeUserIsDecidedAndItsRefusalIsHard`, `TestSSLCannotBeStripped`, `TestALocalInfileRequestIsRefusedHard`, `TestLoadDataLocalIsRefusedHardAndFileIsNot`, `TestCleartextPasswordIsRefusedWithoutEncryption`, `TestARuleWidensTheCommandListForItsOwnTraffic`, `TestADenyListIsNotOverridableByARule`, `TestReadOnlyIncludesTheStatementsThatRunCode`, `TestAnUnreadableStatementIsRefusedHard`, `TestCompileRefusesWhatItCannotUnderstand`; `TestTheDeniedCapabilitiesAreStrippedFromTheGreeting`, `TestAnAdministrativeCommandNeverReachesTheServer`, `TestARefusedStatementNeverReachesTheServer`, `TestAStatementHiddenInAnExecutableCommentIsRefused`, `TestTheServerCannotAskTheClientForAFile`, `TestSetOptionCannotTurnMultiStatementsOn`, `TestChangeUserIsDecidedAgainstTheNewIdentity`, `TestShadowModeForwardsPolicyAndStillRefusesTheHardShapes`, `TestAClientOutsideTheListNeverReachesTheServer`, `TestARuleLetsOneAccountStreamTheBinlog` | The framing's two traps first, because they are the reason the reader exists rather than a loop over io.ReadFull. A payload of exactly 0xffffff means "more follows", so a message whose length is an exact multiple of it ends with an **empty** packet -- asserted directly, because a reader that stopped on "length zero" would end the message one packet early while one that stops on "not full" gets it right. The sequence number is asserted in both places it can go wrong, and the distinction the relay depends on is pinned: strict within a continuation chain, because mis-reassembling would build a message neither peer sent, and lax between messages, because a relay originates packets of its own and so desynchronises exactly when it is doing its job -- while the server enforces the numbering anyway. Then the handshake in both directions, with the cases that must not parse: protocol 9 (the pre-4.0 shape, so a relay that guessed would forward octets it had not read), an error packet where a greeting should be, and a client response without CLIENT_PROTOCOL_41. The short 32-octet form a client sends before its TLS handshake is asserted *recognised as such* rather than as a truncated response, because reading it the other way would refuse every TLS client there is. The capability strip is tested for what it clears and, more importantly, for what it leaves alone: a relay that cleared more than it meant to would break TLS or authentication while looking like it was helping, so CapSSL, PROTOCOL_41, SECURE_CONNECTION, PLUGIN_AUTH and CONNECT_WITH_DB are each asserted to survive. The default command list is held to its claim in both directions -- the thirteen administrative commands absent, the five a driver needs present -- and the weak-plugin judgement likewise, because mysql_native_password being *not* weak is a deliberate line rather than an omission. The dialect tests in sqlkind carry the lexical differences that decide whether a keyword is visible at all: MySQL's `/*! ... */` executable comments are asserted to be **code** (including the semicolon inside one, which really does separate two statements) while the same text in PostgreSQL is a comment; block comments nest in one dialect and not the other, so `/* /* */` is a complete comment in MySQL and unterminated in PostgreSQL; and a backslash escapes a quote in MySQL and does not in PostgreSQL, so `SELECT 'a\'; DROP TABLE t'` is one statement in one and two in the other. A keyword from the wrong dialect is asserted `unknown`, in both directions, because a merged keyword table would make each dialect's relay less strict by exactly the other's vocabulary. Then the relay end to end against a fake MySQL server that greets, accepts a login and records every command and statement that reached it. The strip is verified from the *client's* side -- it reads the relay's edited greeting -- and then verified again from the server's, by asserting the client never negotiated local_files with it. Six administrative commands are shown never to arrive and the connection shown still usable afterwards; a DROP hidden in an executable comment is refused; COM_SET_OPTION turning multi-statements on is refused while turning it off is not; a COM_CHANGE_USER to a user the policy refuses never reaches the server; the server's own request for /etc/passwd is answered with an empty file so nothing of the client's crosses; and shadow mode is shown to forward a soft refusal and still refuse a replication command. Three bugs the tests found: MaxPayload exceeded MaxMessage so any continuation chain was refused (the protocol ceiling and the policy default had been conflated into one constant); the relay's own reader enforced the cross-message sequence it had just desynchronised by forwarding a login; and the "is the authentication exchange over" test was a guess at a sequence number, which meant commands sent early were forwarded unchecked -- it is the server's OK packet now |
