`TestAnAllowedSessionReachesTheServer`, `TestTheRelayAsksTheServerForEncryptionOnItsOwnAccount`, `TestAServerThatWillNotEncryptIsRefused`, `TestThePolicyReachesTheStatementInsideAParameterisedQuery`, `TestAWayOutOfTheDatabaseIsRefusedByDefault`, `TestMonitorModeStillRefusesAShellCommand`, `TestARefusedLoginIsToldSoAndGoesNoFurther`, `TestAMessageTypeOffTheListGoesNoFurther`, `TestAnUnreadableDynamicCallGoesNoFurther`, `TestAPlaintextPasswordIsRefusedEvenInMonitorMode`, `TestTheHandshakeRunsInsideTDSPacketsAndThenStops`, `TestTheTunnelRefusesWhatIsNeitherFraming`, `TestTheTunnelReadsEitherFramingUntilThePeerStops`, `TestAWaitingReadDoesNotBlockAWrite`, `TestTheTunnelBoundsAHandshakePacket`, `TestRequireTLSWithoutACertificateRefusesToStart`, `TestDropSaysNothing`; `TestExecMeansRunAProcedureInTSQLAndRunAPreparedStatementInPostgres` | The framing first, and the one bit that matters: **only** the EOM status bit ends a message, so a short packet does not, and a continuation of a different type is refused because reassembling one would build a message neither peer sent. A length below the header is refused rather than underflowed, which is PostgreSQL's self-referential length in a different encoding. Then the PRELOGIN table, where the assertions are about disagreement: an offset or length that runs past the body is refused, and so is a token that appears **twice** -- because whichever value wins depends on whether a reader keeps the first or the last, and on the ENCRYPTION option that is the entire negotiation. `Deobfuscate` is round-tripped against a known answer so the claim "the password is an encoding, not encryption" is demonstrated rather than asserted in a comment; the relay never calls it. Then `rpc.go`, which is the file the kind's value rests on. The statement inside `sp_executesql` is asserted read, by name and by numeric identifier, because most drivers use the number; the prepare family's statement is asserted found at its *signature's* position rather than at "the first string-shaped argument", which is third for `sp_prepare` and fourth for `sp_cursorprepexec`; a procedure with no statement in its signature is asserted to have none **invented** for it, because a statement appearing out of a call that has none would be a decision about text nobody sent; and the partially length-prefixed form is exercised with a 9000-character statement in both the declared-length and unknown-length shapes, because that is how every statement past 4000 characters arrives. Six ways a dynamic call can be unreadable are each asserted refused, including chunks that outrun their own declared total -- two numbers disagreeing about one value, so neither is believed. The policy tests are led by the downgrade defence: every encryption value a client can ask for, including a nonsense one, is answered `required`, and the "forced" flag is asserted to fire *exactly* when the relay raised the negotiation, because the log line it drives is the list an operator reads before turning `require_tls` on. The converse is asserted too -- a client that asked to encrypt is not talked out of it when the requirement is off, because a relay answering `off` to everybody would be performing the downgrade itself. The dangerous procedure set is asserted refused *and* hard for every member, while a procedure merely off the allow list is asserted **soft**, because that one is most likely an application nobody has listed yet and finding those is what monitor mode is for; an explicit allow is asserted to win over both. End to end against a fake SQL Server that negotiates PRELOGIN, accepts a LOGIN7 and records every message, procedure and statement that reached it -- because a test that checked only the client's error would pass against a relay that answered a refusal and forwarded the message anyway. The same `DELETE` is refused both as a batch and inside an `sp_executesql`, which is the assertion that the two routes reach one policy. The TLS test drives a real crypto/tls client through the TDS encapsulation and then runs the statement policy over the encrypted connection, with the tunnel's either-framing window, its bound and its refusal of a third framing tested directly. Two bugs these found: the tunnel held one mutex across its underlying read, so a write deadlocked behind a read waiting for the peer -- which appears only once both directions are live, so after every test of the handshake had passed; and `sqlkind` classified T-SQL's `EXEC` as the prepared-statement kind, which is right for PostgreSQL and wrong here, because T-SQL has no prepared-statement syntax and its `EXEC` runs a stored procedure. `read_only` was decorative on the dialect where it matters most |
| `internal/respwire`, `internal/kinds/redis` (Redis) | `TestACommandIsReadAndItsNameFolded`, `TestASubcommandIsReadOnTheCommandsWhereItDecides`, `TestTheInlineFormIsRead`, `TestTheCountsAreCheckedBeforeAnythingIsAllocated`, `TestABulkBoundAboveTheMessageBoundIsBroughtDown`, `TestTheMessagesThatAreNotCommands`, `TestTheRawOctetsAreKeptExactly`, `TestAPipelineIsReadOneCommandAtATime`, `TestTheKeysAreFoundWhereTheSignatureSaysTheyAre`, `TestAnOptionDependentKeyPositionIsReportedUnknown`, `TestAKeyCountThatDisagreesWithTheArgumentsIsRefused`, `TestAnUnknownCommandCountsAsAWrite`, `TestTheCommandTablesAgreeWithEachOther`, `TestAnErrorReplyCannotBeSplit`, `TestClipCutsOnARuneBoundary`, `TestSomethingThatCannotBeACommandNameIsRefused`, `TestAKeyIsClippedForTheLogAndNotForTheWire`; `FuzzNext`, `FuzzReadThenForward`, `FuzzError`; `TestTheDefaultListIsWhatAnApplicationDoes`, `TestRefusingAWayOutIsNeverShadowed`, `TestASubcommandCanBeAllowedWithoutItsSiblings`, `TestACommandBeforeAuthenticationIsRefusedHard`, `TestTheKeyPrefixPolicy`, `TestACommandWhoseKeysCannotBeLocatedIsRefusedUnderAPrefixPolicy`, `TestReadOnly`, `TestTheInlineFormIsOffByDefault`, `TestTheAuthIdentityLists`, `TestTLSIsDecidedBeforeACommandIsRead`, `TestSelectIsBoundedWhenTheConfigurationSaysSo`, `TestRulesAndSchedules`, `TestABadConfigurationIsRefusedAtCompile`, `TestTheBoundsReachTheReader`; `TestAnAllowedSessionReachesTheServer`, `TestAWayOutOfTheDatabaseGoesNoFurther`, `TestMonitorModeStillRefusesTheDangerousSet`, `TestAuthenticationIsTakenFromTheServersAnswer`, `TestTheKeyPrefixPolicyEndToEnd`, `TestSomethingThatIsNotRESPIsAnsweredAndTheConnectionEnds`, `TestTheInlineFormIsRefusedByDefaultAndReadToDoIt`, `TestRequireTLSWithoutACertificateRefusesToStart`, `TestMaxCommandsEndsTheConnection`, `TestDropSaysNothing` | The two counts a client chooses come first, each asserted refused *before* anything is sized by it -- and then the bound that actually matters, because a permitted element count of permitted-length strings multiplies past both: sixty-four 200 KB values is a bound met sixty-four times and 12 MB held. A bulk bound configured above the message bound is asserted brought down, since otherwise the message check fires first and the configured value never applies, which reads in a log as the wrong limit refusing the request. The name is asserted folded and the *argument* asserted not, because Redis command names are case-insensitive and its keys are not -- a relay that folded a key would apply a prefix policy to a key the server does not have. Anything that cannot be a command name is refused rather than carried, in both wire forms, because the name reaches a log line, a counter label and a shadow-mode report; module spellings with dots and underscores are asserted to survive that check, or every module in the estate would be refused by it. The key tables get the most attention, because they are the whole of what a key prefix policy rests on. Interleaved keys (`MSET` is key, value, key, value), a final argument that is a timeout rather than a key (`BLPOP`), a container command's key behind its subcommand, and a key count declared in an argument are each asserted at the right position -- and the ten commands whose positions depend on an option are asserted to report that they are *unknown* rather than guessed, while still being known commands, which is the distinction that lets a validator name them and a read-only listener judge them. A key count that disagrees with the arguments is believed in neither direction. An unknown command is asserted to count as a write, which is the direction a classifier has to be wrong in: Redis gains commands every release and a module adds its own. A test asserts the four tables agree with each other rather than a comment claiming it -- everything the default allow list or the dangerous set names must be nameable in a configuration, nothing may be in both, every entry folded. It caught four commands that do not exist (PSETNX, SETGET and two invented `_RO` variants) and two key positions wrong in the direction that matters: SINTERCARD's fixed spec counted its LIMIT token as a key, and ZUNIONSTORE's found the destination and none of the sources. `FuzzReadThenForward` is the invariant a relay rests on -- what the reader keeps as Raw, read again, is the same command -- and it found that the bulk header was recorded twice, so the octets the relay forwarded were not the octets it decided about. The error reply is fuzzed for one property, that it is a single line with no terminator inside it: its terminator is its framing, so a CR in the text is a second reply the relay never meant to send, built out of a string a client chose. Then the relay end to end against a fake Redis that records every command and key that reached it, because a test checking only the client's error would pass against a relay that answered a refusal and forwarded the command anyway. Authentication is asserted taken from the *server's* reply: a wrong password is sent, refused, and the next command asserted still refused -- a relay that believed the attempt would have treated it as a login. The `CONFIG SET dir` chain, `MODULE LOAD`, `EVAL`, `REPLICAOF`, `FLUSHALL`, `KEYS` and `MONITOR` are each shown never to arrive, with the connection asserted still usable afterwards -- a relay that closed on the first refusal would turn a refused command into an outage. Monitor mode is shown forwarding a write and a merely-unlisted command while still refusing all of those. One bug the race detector found: the accept loop's WaitGroup Add raced its shutdown Wait, which is not a detector warning so much as a session that either is or is not waited for depending on the scheduler. Fixed in `internal/acceptgroup`, adopted by four kinds, with its own test that runs Enter against Close and Wait two hundred times |
| `internal/kinds/modbus` (value semantics) | `TestTheValueBoundsThatAreAboutAChange` (delta, on_unknown, transitions, rate, select-before-operate, masked writes, coils, the bound); `TestModbusValueSemanticsEndToEnd` | The four bounds that are about a change rather than a value, each tested for the thing that makes it a control rather than a decoration. A delta is measured against the last value the relay *saw*, so the test feeds it one through a read, nudges inside the bound, jumps outside it, and then proves the allowed write became the new baseline -- a delta always measured from the first value ever seen would refuse a slow ramp. With `on_unknown: refuse` a write before any value is known is refused and the check that ran without one is counted; with the default it is allowed and the range still holds, which is the fallback the warning at validation is about. A transition list permits what it names and nothing else, and the wildcard side is exercised because `*->0` is how a stop is written. A rate is per address and per unit, so the third write inside the minute is refused while another address and another device are not. Select-before-operate is refused with no select, is **not** armed by a read that happens to find the select register at the right value -- a poll is not somebody confirming an operation -- and passes once the select is written. A masked write makes the relay forget the address rather than guess, so the next delta says it does not know. A coil goes through the same machinery as a value of 0 or 1. And the table is bounded, dropping what it cannot hold and counting it. End to end through a real relay, the rule from the documentation: the setpoint with no permissive is refused with illegal data value and never reaches the device, the permissive then the setpoint arrives, and the second write inside the minute is refused with *server busy* -- a different exception on purpose, because the same write would be accepted later |
| `internal/amqpwire`, `internal/kinds/amqp` (AMQP 0-9-1 and 1.0) | `TestTheHeaderSaysWhichProtocol`, `TestSomethingThatIsNotAMQPIsRefusedAsSuch`, `TestFramesAreReadAndBounded`, `TestAFrameThatDoesNotEndWhereItSaidIsRefused`, `TestAFrameOverTheBoundIsRefusedBeforeItIsRead`, `TestATruncatedFrameIsAnError`, `TestTheTenFramingIsReadWithItsDataOffset`, `TestTheTenFramingRefusesAnImpossibleHeader`, `TestAFrameBeforeTheVersionIsKnownIsRefused`, `TestAnUnknownFrameTypeIsReportedRatherThanGuessedAt`; `TestEachMethodNamesWhatAPolicyDecidesOn`, `TestAMethodThatDoesNotLayOutIsNotGuessedAt`, `TestAMethodFrameShorterThanItsClassIsRefused`, `TestTheIdentityIsReadAndTheSecretIsNot`, `TestTheHandshakeFieldsAreRead`; `TestEveryFieldTypeIsSteppedOverExactly`, `TestAnUnknownFieldTypeMakesTheTableUnreadable`, `TestANestedTableIsBounded`, `TestATableThatRunsPastItsLengthIsRefused`, `TestTheLoginTableIsReadWithoutItsLength`, `TestTheContentHeaderIsReadForWhatAPolicyNeeds`, `TestAContentHeaderThatDoesNotLayOutIsRefused`, `TestTheCatalogueAndTheClassificationsAgree`; `TestTheTenPerformativesAreRead`, `TestTheSASLLayerIsRead`, `TestADescriptorWrittenAsASymbolIsTheSamePerformative`, `TestAFrameBodyThatIsNotAPerformativeIsRefused`, `TestTheTypeReaderIsBounded`, `TestAnAbsentFieldIsNotZero`, `TestALinkAddressIsReadIntoTheNounsAPolicyUses`, `TestThePerformativeCatalogueIsConsistent`; `FuzzFrames`, `FuzzMethod`, `FuzzPerformative`; `TestTheDefaultAllowsWorkAndNotTopology`, `TestTheDestructiveMethodsAreNeverShadowed`, `TestTheHiddenExchangeArgumentsAreChecked`, `TestTheNameListsAreGlobsThatDoNotCrossASeparator`, `TestTheManagementNodesAreRefusedByDefault`, `TestTheProtocolVersionIsDecidedBeforeAnythingElse`, `TestTheMechanismAndTheIdentityAreDecidedSeparately`, `TestTheNegotiatedBoundsAreDecided`, `TestTheContentPolicy`, `TestTheInboundDirectionAppliesOnlyTheDenyLists`, `TestTheOnePolicyDecidesBothVersions`, `TestAnOperationBeforeAuthenticationIsRefused`, `TestARuleWidensForItsOwnTraffic`, `TestTheDefaultActionDecidesWhenNoRuleMatched`, `TestACompileErrorIsAConfigurationError`, `TestAnUnknownMethodIsRefusedRatherThanForwarded`, `TestNoAckCanBeRefused`; `TestAnAllowedSessionReachesTheBroker`, `TestATopologyMethodIsRefusedAndNeverReachesTheBroker`, `TestADeadLetterExchangeOutsideThePolicyIsRefused`, `TestAnOversizeMessageIsRefusedBeforeItsBody`, `TestAnOperationBeforeTheBrokerAnsweredIsRefused`, `TestACredentialTheBrokerRefusesIsNotALogin`, `TestAnAnonymousLoginIsRefused`, `TestARefusedVersionIsAnsweredWithASupportedHeader`, `TestADeliveryFromADeniedExchangeIsRefused`, `TestATenConnectionIsRelayedAndDecided`, `TestATenAttachToTheManagementNodeIsRefused`, `TestATenTransferOnAnUnknownHandleIsRefused`, `TestATenConnectionWithoutSASLIsRefused`, `TestADeniedMethodIsRefusedWhateverTheMode`, `TestATenMessageIsBoundedAcrossItsTransfers`, `TestClosedChannelsAndDetachedLinksAreForgotten`, `TestATenCredentialTheBrokerRefusesIsNotALogin`, `TestTheAccessLogNamesWhatAnOperationNamed`, `TestTheListenerTerminatesTLSAndThenSpeaksAMQP` | Two protocols share a port, so the first thing tested is telling them apart: each version's header, the two revisions before 0-9-1 (whose version fields are the other way round, which is the trap), 1.0's three protocol identifiers, and four things that are not AMQP at all. Then the framing of each, with the malformed frames that are the reason the reader exists: a 0-9-1 frame whose end octet is not where its size said -- which means the stream is no longer framed and every octet after it is the sender's choice of what this relay reads as a method -- a frame claiming four gigabytes, which is refused against the declared size before anything is allocated, every truncation of a good frame, and a 1.0 frame whose data offset points inside its own header or past its end. The argument layouts are driven one method at a time against the frames a client really sends, asserting the *targets in full*, because a missing target is a name nothing checks: the exchange of a publish, the queue of a consume, and the two that a policy written against the obvious fields would miss -- `x-dead-letter-exchange` on a queue and `alternate-exchange` on an exchange, each of which names an exchange the broker will route to. Every truncation of the fields a method's targets come from is asserted to report `known` false rather than a shorter list of names, and a frame with *more* arguments than this package reads is asserted to still lay out, because a broker may add a field and refusing the connection over one would be refusing an upgrade. The field table gets its own set, one entry of every type code with the two text entries at the end so any type skipped by the wrong width loses them -- and the specification and RabbitMQ disagree about `s`, so a table that does not lay out is asserted unreadable rather than half read, which is what stops a policy checking a list against whatever survived. `TestTheIdentityIsReadAndTheSecretIsNot` drives PLAIN in its three forms, RabbitMQ's AMQPLAIN, EXTERNAL and ANONYMOUS, and asserts the password never comes back in the identity. The 1.0 type reader is bounded three ways and each is tested: nesting past the depth bound, a compound whose count says four billion in a twelve-octet frame, and a frame describing more values than the budget -- and `TestAnAbsentFieldIsNotZero` pins the distinction a relay would otherwise get wrong, since an absent `idle-time-out` is not a heartbeat of zero. Three fuzz targets drove the frame reader, the 0-9-1 layouts and the 1.0 type reader for about two minutes each, fifteen million executions with no crashers, asserting that nothing panics, a header is handed back as the octets it arrived as, and nothing comes out larger than what went in. Then the policy, where the default posture is the assertion: an application can publish, consume and acknowledge, and cannot declare, delete, bind, unbind or purge -- with the destructive three asserted hard, so monitor mode does not forward a purge in order to write it down, while a declare stays soft because a trial run is what the mode is for. The name lists are the protocol's own topic language, so `orders.*` is asserted to cover `orders.created` and not `orders.eu.created`, and the default exchange -- named by the empty string -- is asserted to need naming deliberately. Then the relay end to end through the real engine against a broker that records what reached it, because a test checking only the client's error would pass against a relay that refused and forwarded anyway: every refusal is asserted twice, once on the client and once on the broker. The 0-9-1 handshake completes as a client library does it, a topology method never arrives, a dead-letter exchange outside the policy is refused while the same declare with one inside it goes through, a message is refused on the size its content header declared before its body arrives, an operation before the broker answered is refused, and a version this listener does not serve is answered with one it does -- with the broker never dialled at all. The 1.0 side runs the SASL layer, the second protocol header both peers send afterwards, and an attach whose address the policy decides; a transfer on a handle nobody attached is refused, because the address behind a handle is what was checked; a message split across four transfers is bounded by their sum, since bounding each frame would bound nothing; and a 1.0 client that skips the SASL layer entirely -- a connection with no identity at all -- gets nowhere. `TestTheListenerTerminatesTLSAndThenSpeaksAMQP` runs a real handshake, because `require_tls` is the setting that matters most on a protocol whose PLAIN mechanism is the username and the password in one field |
| `internal/s7`, `internal/kinds/s7` (Siemens S7comm) | `TestFramesAreReadAndBounded`, `TestTheFramingRefusesWhatCannotBeAFrame`, `TestTheConnectionRequestCarriesTheRackAndSlot`, `TestTheCOTPHeaderRefusesWhatItCannotRead`, `TestTheS7HeaderIsRead`, `TestTheNegotiatedPDULengthIsRead`, `TestTheControlServiceNameIsRead`, `TestTheBlockADownloadNamesIsRead`; `TestTheItemsOfAReadAreRead`, `TestTheValuesOfAWriteAreMatchedToTheirItems`, `TestAnItemThatIsNotAnAddressSaysSo`, `TestItemsThatDoNotLayOutAreRefusedRatherThanShortened`, `TestTheAreaAndTransportNamesRoundTrip`; `TestTheUserDataLayerIsRead`, `TestUserDataThatDoesNotLayOutIsNotAnOperation`, `TestEveryFunctionCodeMapsOntoTheVocabulary`, `TestTheVocabularyIsConsistent`; `FuzzFrames`; `TestTheDefaultReadsAndChangesNothing`, `TestWhatChangesThePLCIsNeverShadowed`, `TestReadOnlyCannotBeWidenedByARule`, `TestTheAddressPolicyCoversTheWholeSpan`, `TestTheWriteRangeIsSeparateFromTheReadRange`, `TestTheConnectionRequestDecidesWhichControllerIsReached`, `TestTheBoundsAreEnforced`, `TestWhatCannotBeReadIsRefused`, `TestTheBlockTypePolicy`, `TestARuleNarrowsAndWidensForItsOwnTraffic`, `TestACompileErrorIsAConfigurationError`; `TestAnAllowedSessionReachesTheController`, `TestAWriteIsRefusedAndNeverReachesTheController`, `TestAStopIsRefused`, `TestAWrongSlotIsRefusedBeforeTheControllerIsDialled`, `TestAProgrammingDeviceIsRefusedOnAnOperatorListener`, `TestMemoryOutsideThePolicyIsRefused`, `TestTheItemBoundIsEnforced`, `TestAnUnknownFunctionIsRefusedAndNotForwarded`, `TestAnUnknownTransportTypeEndsTheConnection`, `TestSettingTheClockIsRefusedInItsOwnLayer`, `TestReadOnlyHoldsThroughTheEngine`, `TestMonitorModeStillRefusesTheOperationsThatChangeThePLC`, `TestAnUploadIsOffUntilNamed`, `TestAnOversizePDUNegotiationIsRefused`, `TestDenyResponseCloseEndsTheConnection`, `TestTheControllersOwnRefusalIsRecorded`, `TestARateLimitedRequestIsRefusedAndTheSessionCarriesOn`, `TestARefusalCounts` | Three layers have to be read before a policy exists, so the framing is tested as three: a TPKT length that disagrees with the frame, a COTP header whose length octet points past its own parameters, and an S7 PDU whose parameter and data lengths do not add up to what arrived. Each is refused rather than shortened, and the bounds are asserted *before* an allocation, because the length in a TPKT header is the sender's choice. `TestTheConnectionRequestCarriesTheRackAndSlot` is the one that matters most, since the called TSAP is where the whole address policy comes from: the resource, the rack and the slot are asserted for each of the three connection types, and a request with no called TSAP at all is asserted *unaddressed* rather than defaulted to rack 0 slot 0 -- a default there would silently admit a client to the first CPU in the rack. The item specifications get their own set, because an item is where a read names memory: the protocol carries a **bit** address and a policy is written in bytes, so the conversion is pinned in both directions, a write's values are matched to their items across the odd-length fill octet that would otherwise shift every value after the first, and an item written in the symbolic syntax rather than S7ANY is asserted to report *not addressable* instead of being read as some address. The vocabulary is the join between the two layers, so `TestEveryFunctionCodeMapsOntoTheVocabulary` drives every documented function code and every user-data group against the word it becomes, and `TestTheVocabularyIsConsistent` asserts the nineteen words, the default set and the writing set agree with each other -- which is what makes `read_only` mean the same thing as the sum of its parts. `FuzzFrames` ran the whole stack, TPKT through item lists, for four and a half million executions with no crashers. Then the policy, where the default posture is the assertion: an HMI reads memory, the status lists, the block list, the clock and a cyclic subscription, and nothing changes the controller -- with every changing operation asserted **hard**, so monitor mode does not forward a stop in order to write it down, while a read of an unlisted data block stays soft because a trial run on a plant nobody has an inventory of is what the mode is for. `TestTheAddressPolicyCoversTheWholeSpan` pins the rule that a range applies to the whole span a request covers: a read starting inside a range and ending outside it is refused, not clipped. Then the relay end to end through the real engine against a controller that records every operation that reached it, because a test checking only the client's answer would pass against a relay that refused a write and forwarded it anyway -- which on this protocol moves an actuator. Every refusal is asserted twice, once on the client and once on the PLC. A wrong slot and a programming-device connection on an operator listener are each asserted to leave the controller having seen **nothing at all**, which is the pre-dial ordering: a client that may not reach a CPU must not take one of its sixteen connection resources. A write is refused with error class `0x87` and the next read still goes through, since a refusal must not turn into an outage on a poll loop; setting the clock is refused in the user-data layer with its own group and subfunction rather than as an acknowledgement, because that is where the client looks; `read_only` holds against a rule that names `write` and `download`; and a rate limit is asserted on the counter and then on the controller, because that refusal has no answer to read
| `test/docs` (the per-protocol pages) | `TestEveryKindHasAProtocolPage`, `TestNoPageNamesAKindThatDoesNotExist`, `TestEveryPageIsComplete`, `TestEveryPageLinksToItsSettings`, `TestTheIndexNamesEveryPage`, `TestTheIndexIsLinkedFromTheDocsAPersonStartsAt` | docs/CONFIG.md is checked against the schema by reflection, so a setting cannot go undocumented; nothing did that for a *protocol*, and a kind could be registered, rostered, configured and shipped without anybody writing down what the protocol is or what security it was designed with. So the pages are tied to the roster in both directions: every kind has one, and no page describes a kind the roster does not name. The part that stops a page being a stub is the section check -- each of the five headings has to be present, in order, with at least two hundred characters under it, because a page whose sections are all empty passes a test that only looks for headings and is worse than no page at all, since a reader has spent their trust by the time they find out. `TestEveryPageLinksToItsSettings` resolves the anchor rather than matching a string, and that is the one that earned its place: docs/CONFIG.md spells its per-kind headings two ways -- twenty-two older kinds as `### server.listeners[].<kind>` and the seven newest as `## <kind>` -- so the anchor a page needs is not derivable from the kind name, and every link written from the obvious guess rendered as a link that silently went nowhere. Twenty of them did, until this test said so. The index is checked for a link to every page and for naming all three daemons, and the three documents a person actually starts at are checked for a link to the index, because a directory of pages nobody links to is a directory nobody reads |
| `internal/schedule` (the shared time window) | `TestADayIsReadInBothSpellings`, `TestTheSpellingsAgreeWithValidation`, `TestANilWindowIsAlwaysInForce`, `TestAWindowWithinOneDay`, `TestAWindowWithNoTimesIsTheWholeDay`, `TestAWindowWithNoDaysIsEveryDay`, `TestAWindowSpanningMidnightBelongsToTheDayItStartedOn`, `TestTheOldReadingIsGone`, `TestTwoNamedNightsEachReachIntoTheirMorning`, `TestTheWindowIsReadInItsOwnTimezone`, `TestWhatCannotBeCompiled`, `TestMinutes` | Thirteen kinds had their own copy of this, so the tests are written against what the copies disagreed about rather than against the happy path. `TestTheSpellingsAgreeWithValidation` drives every day name `internal/config` accepts through `Compile` and is the assertion that stops the drift returning: twelve copies accepted only `mon` while validation accepted `monday` too, so a file passed `-validate` and then refused to start. `TestAWindowSpanningMidnightBelongsToTheDayItStartedOn` pins the semantics that had two answers in the tree, and `TestTheOldReadingIsGone` asserts both halves of the change -- Friday's own small hours out, Saturday's in -- because a fix that only closed the window nobody wrote would have left the one they did write closed too. `TestTwoNamedNightsEachReachIntoTheirMorning` is the case that catches an off-by-one-day in the yesterday lookup: naming Friday and Saturday has to cover Sunday morning and not Monday's. The timezone test drives the same local window in January and in July, because a window flattened to UTC at compile time would be an hour wrong for half the year. `TestWhatCannotBeCompiled` includes `+6:00` and `006:00`, which is why the clock field is read by hand rather than with `strconv.Atoi` -- a signed or padded hour that one reader accepts and another does not is the same class of bug the package was written to end. Before the migration, an equivalence harness drove the new package and each old copy over 48,000 (window, moment) pairs and confirmed the only disagreements were the midnight-spanning ones: 84 of 3,528, none outside them |
| `internal/numrange` (the shared range list) | `TestTheSpellingsAnEngineerWrites`, `TestWhatIsRefused`, `TestTheErrorNamesTheSetting`, `TestCoversNeedsOneRangeToHoldTheWholeSpan`, `TestHasAndContains`, `TestStringRoundTrips`, `TestParseNum` | The spellings are driven from what a register map actually looks like: decimal, `0x10-0x1F`, whitespace picked up from a YAML list written over several lines, and a range of one. `TestCoversNeedsOneRangeToHoldTheWholeSpan` is the one that matters, because it pins the conservative reading both kinds relied on: `0-99` and `100-199` do not together permit a request for 50 to 150, since an operator who wrote two ranges described two regions and splitting a straddling request would be the relay deciding which half they meant. `TestWhatIsRefused` includes `-5` and `5-`, which is why the separator is found with `IndexByte` at a position greater than zero rather than with `Cut` -- a leading minus has to reach the bound check as a negative number rather than become an empty low end. `TestStringRoundTrips` asserts a parsed range renders back as what was typed, which is what makes the modbus kind's learned output a file somebody can paste into a policy. 100% coverage |
| `internal/sesslimit` (the shared session bound) | `TestTheZeroGateIsUnbounded`, `TestTheGlobalBound`, `TestThePerClientBound`, `TestTheGlobalBoundIsCheckedFirst`, `TestTheTableDoesNotGrow`, `TestALeaveWithoutAnEnterCannotGoNegative`, `TestTheBoundHoldsUnderConcurrentAccepts`, `TestTheGlobalBoundHoldsUnderConcurrentAccepts`, `TestAnEnterRacingALeaveIsStillCounted`; and `TestThePerClientSessionBoundHoldsUnderParallelDials` in `internal/kinds/s7` | The two concurrency tests are the reason the package exists, and the shape they are written in is part of the test rather than a stylistic choice: the goroutines spin on an atomic flag instead of waiting on a WaitGroup, because the window between the old check and the old act was a couple of instructions wide and a barrier that parks and wakes 512 goroutines spreads their arrival out far enough to hide it. Spinning reproduced the defect in every run; parking reproduced it in none. Both bounds are driven, because both had the fault. `TestAnEnterRacingALeaveIsStillCounted` covers the second one -- the release path deleting a client's entry while somebody held the counter it fetched -- over two thousand rounds, asserting that the live count and the table are both empty afterwards. `TestTheTableDoesNotGrow` is the leak the per-client map would otherwise be: one entry per address that has ever connected is a table an attacker fills for free. `TestALeaveWithoutAnEnterCannotGoNegative` covers the double-release a deferred call written twice produces, since a count below zero would make room that does not exist. The end-to-end test in the s7 kind asserts the bound is wired to the right setting and counted under the reason an operator watches; it deliberately does **not** claim to catch the race -- removing the lock leaves it passing, because a real dial and handshake take long enough that accepts do not collide. That is stated in the test so nobody mistakes it for the guard. 100% coverage under `-race` |

`TestAReadReachesTheDeviceAndTheAnswerComesBack`, `TestTwoClientsWithTheSameInvokeIdentifierEachGetTheirOwnAnswer`, `TestAWriteIsRefusedByTheDefaultServiceList`, `TestAnAllowedWriteReachesThePlant`, `TestAWriteAtLifeSafetyPriorityIsRefused`, `TestAWriteAtAnOrdinaryPriorityIsCarried`, `TestAWriteToOutOfServiceIsRefused`, `TestAForeignDeviceRegistrationIsRefused`, `TestABroadcastsAnswersAreBounded`, `TestAnUnsolicitedReplyIsNotDelivered`, `TestAClientOutsideTheListIsRefusedWithoutReadingItsRequest`, `TestSomethingElseOnThePortDoesNotReachTheDevice`, `TestARequestWhoseObjectCannotBeFoundIsRefusedWhenObjectRulesExist`, `TestShadowModeCarriesAPolicyRefusalAndStillHoldsTheBounds`, `TestAGlobalBroadcastIsRefused`, `TestARoutedRequestToANamedNetworkIsCarried` | The declared length is asserted refused in *both* directions, and the short case is the one that matters: octets past the declared end are octets no BACnet device reads and this relay therefore does not inspect, which is a way to carry something past it. The reserved bits of the network and application headers are each asserted refused, because a sender that sets one is arranging for two readers to disagree. The addressing fields are asserted read in the standard's order, since the hop count follows the *source* address and not the destination's, which is the classic parser bug here. Where each service keeps its object gets the most attention, because a policy about objects rests on it: a COV notification is asserted to yield the object that changed rather than the device that sent it, `subscribeCOV` to skip the process identifier that is not an object at all, an access list to yield every object and every property rather than the first of them, and an array index next to a property identifier not to be read as a property. The services whose object no fixed position describes are asserted to report that they could not be located -- distinctly from the services that genuinely name none -- because a listener with object rules must refuse the first and carry the second, and a missing object where one belongs is asserted to be the first case rather than the second. `FuzzParse` ran twenty million executions and found one defect: a four-octet property identifier decodes to a number and only some of those numbers are property identifiers, so a value past the twenty-two bits clause 21 gives one is now refused where it is read. A test asserts the service tables agree with each other rather than a comment claiming it -- nothing in the default allow list may write or be dangerous, everything dangerous must be a write or one of the three withdrawn services, every name must parse back to itself -- and the same test asserts that confirmed choice 6 and unconfirmed choice 6 are different services, which is the reason a policy cannot match on the number. Then the relay end to end against a fake controller that records every datagram that reached it, because a test checking only the client's rejection would pass against a relay that refused the client and forwarded the request anyway. Two clients using the same invoke identifier towards the same device are asserted each to get their own answer, which is what the identifier translation is for. The test for the bounds in shadow mode found a real ordering defect: the priority bound was hidden behind the service refusal found first, so a listener in monitor mode carried a write at a life safety command slot. `Decide` now matches the rule, then the bounds, then the policy choices. Two more the review found: a listener with `allow_bbmd` on forwarded a table read and dropped the answer, because the link layer carries no identifier to pair one by -- such a request now gets a window of exactly one reply, asserted end to end -- and an unconfirmed request arriving *from* the building was fanned out unchecked, so a device wearing a plant address could broadcast a `timeSynchronization` at a client network and set the clock on every host that listens. The service list applies in that direction too now, with the test asserting the clock broadcast is dropped and an `i-Am` still arrives. And the deepest of the four: a client's segment acknowledgement was forwarded carrying the client's own invoke identifier, which the device does not know, so every segmented reply stalled after its first window -- a trend log download that never arrives. The test drives a whole segmented exchange through a fake device that *checks* the identifier on the acknowledgement it receives, which is the only way a test here could have caught it: an end-to-end test that only read the first segment would have passed. `TestServeAndShutdownDoNotRaceOnStartup` starts and shuts the listener down two hundred times under `-race`: this kind runs two goroutines and uses `internal/acceptgroup` for them rather than a bare WaitGroup, for the reason that package exists -- a listener can be shut down at the moment it starts, and the failure is not a warning but a reader goroutine that either is or is not waited for depending on the scheduler |
| `test/shutdown` (every kind, shut down under load) | `TestShutdownDoesNotRaceArrivingClients`, `TestEveryKindIsCoveredOrExcluded` | Twenty-three listener kinds are started through the real engine, dialled by twenty-four goroutines that keep arriving, and shut down while they do -- under the race detector. It was written for the accept-group sweep and earned its place on the first run, catching the Add/Wait race in `ntske` that no per-kind test was looking for, then `snmp`, `ntp`, `syslog` and `udp` on the runs after that. It also found two things the sweep was not about: `proxy.New` writes the process-wide panic sink as a plain package variable, which a reload writes while flow goroutines may be reading it, and the UDP kind publishes a session into its table *before* it dials the upstream -- deliberately, so a flood of first datagrams cannot each start a dial -- and then wrote three plain fields under everything that could already reach it. What this test does **not** do is catch that window from outside on demand: twenty runs of twenty-four dialers against the unfixed telnet kind never hit it, because it is a few instructions wide and needs the counter at exactly zero. That is written down in the package comment, because a test that looks like it covers a race and does not is worse than one that admits what it covers. What it reliably catches is the other half of getting this wrong, and the risk of changing twenty listeners at once: a shutdown that **hangs**. An Enter without its Leave, or a Close that never happens, leaves the counter above zero for ever, so every kind is asserted to shut down cleanly inside a bound. `TestEveryKindIsCoveredOrExcluded` keeps the table honest: a kind is started here or listed with the reason it is not (four are -- the HTTP plane and the forward proxy have no accept loop of their own, `dns` has its own lifecycle test, and starting `ssh` needs four key files on disk) |
| `internal/acceptgroup` (waiting for a listener's sessions) | `TestWaitWaitsForTheSessionsThatEntered`, `TestAfterCloseASessionIsRefused`, `TestWaitIsBoundedByItsContext`, `TestEnterDoesNotRaceCloseAndWait` | The package exists because the obvious way to write this is wrong, so the test that matters is the last one: four goroutines Entering while another Closes and Waits, two hundred times, under `-race`. A bare `sync.WaitGroup` fails it -- Add must not run concurrently with Wait at zero -- and the failure in production is not a warning but a session that either is or is not waited for depending on the scheduler, so shutdown returns while a session is still reading a connection the process is about to close. The other three pin the contract the kinds depend on: Wait blocks while sessions run and returns when they leave; a session that Enters after Close is refused so the accept loop closes it rather than starting one nothing waits for; and Wait is bounded by its context, because a session blocked on a peer that has stopped reading would otherwise hold the process open for as long as the peer cared to. 100% of statements |

The weakest method is driven as well as the strongest, since forty bit RC4 is what the oldest equipment has, and a desktop that asks for no encryption at all is served without a key exchange happening behind anybody's back. Underneath, a sealed packet does not carry its payload in clear, a signature refuses a packet with any byte flipped in it, and the fast path's one and two byte length forms are rewritten correctly across the signature that changes a unit's size -- including at the boundary where the shorter form no longer fits. The desktop's half of these session tests uses this repository's own code, so they prove the two halves fit and the session is wired correctly; they are **not** an interoperability test against Windows, which needs a Windows desktop. The server half is driven the same way from the other side: an old client reads the gateway's certificate, **verifies its signature with the public half of the published key exactly as a real client does**, seals its random under the key it found and then speaks encrypted -- so a certificate this gateway builds wrongly fails here rather than in front of somebody's mstsc. Then the gateway against a real RFB target: a session reaches the desktop and the stream runs both ways; a viewer picking a type outside the offered list is refused and counted and the desktop is never dialled; a vendor's own type is refused at load with a message naming it as such; the client proves the gateway's password while the gateway answers the desktop's with another, and the wrong one never reaches the desktop; 3.3 and 3.8 reach each other in both directions, and a 3.3 viewer offered only VeNCrypt is refused in the form 3.3 understands rather than left waiting; `view_only` drops key, pointer and cut text while an update request passes, and a message the gateway cannot frame ends the session; the recording carries what the desktop showed with the framebuffer size and the stream named in its header; a client outside `allow_clients` is never spoken to. MS-Logon II is held to the same standard as the specified types even though the type protects nothing: both ends of the exchange derive the same secret and the credential one seals is the one the other opens, the two fields are chained separately so a password does not depend on the username in front of it, a credential over its fixed field is refused rather than cut in half, and every parameter that fixes the shared secret -- a modulus or generator or public value of 0 or 1, or a value at or above the modulus -- is refused on both legs, end to end as well as in the parser. Through a real gateway: a wrong password and a wrong code each leave the desktop untouched, the factor rides the type with no certificate involved, the desktop is opened with the operator's credential and never the person's, a half-configured upstream credential is refused at load, and enabling the type produces advice that says what it is worth. RealVNC's RSA-AES rests on EAX, which the standard library does not have, so that is pinned first and on its own: CMAC against the four vectors of NIST SP 800-38B appendix D, then EAX against the vectors of the EAX paper, and a message whose ciphertext, tag, nonce, header or length was changed opens under none of them. Then the exchange: a key round-trips through the form the transcript is hashed over; a peer key under 2048 bits, over 8192, one whose stated length disagrees with its modulus and one with an even exponent are all refused before any arithmetic; the two directions get different session keys and a 256 bit key is not a 128 bit one extended; the transcript differs by direction so one end's cannot be replayed as the other's; the channel carries a stream in both directions across message boundaries, refuses a message it cannot authenticate rather than handing back the plaintext, and bounds a framed length before allocating for it; a random of the wrong size, one that does not decrypt and an oversize length are each refused. Through a real gateway, for rsa-aes and rsa-aes-256: the session opens and the desktop's stream arrives inside the channel, a wrong password is refused and the desktop untouched, the factor rides the type, a transcript over keys nobody sent ends the exchange, the target's key is pinned so an unpinned one never receives the credential, rsa-aes-ne leaves the channel after the security result and not before, and both the missing key and the missing pin are refused at load. Tight and ARD close the set. Tight's capability lists round-trip and a count of four billion is refused before anything is allocated for it, in both the security list and the block after ServerInit. End to end: a Tight client reaches the desktop and is told there are no extensions, a password makes it settle on the DES challenge and a wrong one never reaches the desktop, an authentication outside the offer is refused, a Tight target's block after ServerInit is swallowed rather than passed to a client that never negotiated Tight, and a target offering only tunnels is not used at all. ARD's two ends derive the same key and the credential one seals is the one the other opens while neither half is on the wire in clear; a generator or public value of 1, a public value at the modulus, an even modulus and a prime shorter than Apple's own are each refused, in the parser and again through a real gateway, where a target with such parameters never receives the credential |
| `internal/netutil` `TestHostHasOneSpellingPerName`, `TestClientHelloSNIHasOneSpellingPerName`; `internal/router` `TestHostSpellingsSelectOneRoute`; `test/bypass` `TestHostSpellings` | Fourth audit round, one name one key: five spellings of a host (root dot, case, port) normalise to the same routing key while an empty label anywhere is refused; the same for a server name peeked from a ClientHello; every spelling of a host reaches its own route rather than the catch-all; end to end, the deny-all route answers 403 under every valid spelling and 400 under every invalid one, and the backend never sees the request |
| `internal/dns` `TestLabelWithADotIsRefused`, `TestControlBytesInALabelAreRefused` | Fourth audit round, DNS names: a label containing a dot is refused, so two wire names cannot share one string key, while the ordinary spelling still parses; NUL, newline, carriage return, space, escape, backslash and DEL in a label are refused while `www`, `_dmarc`, punycode and hyphenated labels parse |
| `internal/jwt` `TestIntrospectionRequiresIssuerAndAudience` | Fourth audit round, introspection: against a real endpoint, an active answer with no issuer, with no audience, and with another relying party's audience are all refused, and a complete answer passes |
| `internal/cluster` `TestPeerIdentityIsTheCertificateName`, `TestCertNameMatches`; `internal/limits` `TestPeerReportCannotExceedThePolicyRate`; `internal/ban` `TestPeerBanLifetimeIsClamped`, `TestPeerCannotRemoveAnOperatorBan` | Fourth audit round, a hostile peer inside the trust boundary: a peer's actions are recorded under its certificate name, not the node id it announced, with the id as a fallback only when there is no certificate; the announced id matches a certificate name case-insensitively and nothing else; a rate report of 1e12 counts as the policy rate and no worse, the key recovers when the report goes stale, and a negative rate credits nothing; a peer ban dated a century out is clamped to the one-year ceiling; a peer cannot remove a ban an operator placed but can remove its own |
| `internal/logging` `TestSIEMNeutralisesControlBytes`, `TestLEEFAttributeCannotOverwriteAReservedField`, `TestSIEMUserFieldNamesRealKeys` | Fourth audit round, SIEM records: NUL, escape, bell and DEL are rendered printable in both CEF and LEEF while the escape byte is still visible as `\x1b`; an attribute called `name` is renamed rather than overwriting the LEEF event name; `suser` and `usrName` are filled from `auth_user`, `api_key` and `oidc_user` |
| `internal/challenge` `TestClientFaultCoversProviderOutages` | Fourth audit round, the defences turned on their users: a full verification table, a spent nonce, a CAPTCHA provider that is unreachable or erroring, and any reason nobody has written yet all fail to count against the client, while the twelve reasons that describe something the client did wrong still do |
| Fuzz: `internal/netutil` `FuzzReadProxyHeader` (invariant corrected) | Fourth audit round, a null result worth recording: the fuzzer broke the target's own assertion that a PROXY header's two addresses share a family. A dual-stack balancer with an IPv6 listener legitimately reports an IPv4 client as `::ffff:a.b.c.d` beside an IPv6 destination, and unmapping that source is what makes an access list match, so the parser was right and the assertion was wrong. The target now asserts what matters: no mapped or zoned form escapes, because the source address becomes the key for access lists, bans and rate limits |
| Fuzz: `internal/netutil` `FuzzClientHelloSNI`; `internal/expr` `FuzzParse`; `internal/ldap` `FuzzReadTLV`, `FuzzParseFilter`; `internal/cluster` `FuzzReadLine` | Fourth audit round, parsers that had no fuzz target. The ClientHello one asserts the properties a routing key must have (bounded length, folded case, no separator or control byte, no empty label) and found the host-spelling defect in twelve seconds; the others assert that a parse either errors or returns something within its stated bounds |
| `internal/dns` `TestNSECRdataShorterThanItsName`, `TestRRSIGRdataShorterThanItsSigner`, `TestSOANameTooLongToRepack`, `TestEmptyNSEC3SetIsNotIndexed`; `internal/netutil` `TestClientHelloExactlyThirtyFour`; `internal/safe` `TestGuardContainsAPanicAndReportsIt`, `TestGuardIsSilentWithoutAPanic`, `TestGuardWithoutAReporter` | Third audit round, panic reachability: the 31-byte datagram whose NSEC rdata is shorter than its own name is an error, the same shape for an RRSIG signer, a name too long to repack never yields rdata that cannot be canonicalised, an NSEC3 set with no records is bogus rather than insecure; every truncation of a 34-byte ClientHello body is refused; the flow guard contains a nil dereference on another goroutine, counts it, reports the stack, costs nothing on a clean return and still counts without a reporter |
| `internal/filters/sensitive` `TestStreamedDecodeBoundsRatio`, `TestStreamedDecodePassesOrdinaryBodies`, `TestMaxDecompressionRatioValidation` | Third audit round, decompression bombs: a body expanding past `max_decompression_ratio` stops with a bomb error, ordinary compressible bodies at ratios well inside the bound are scanned whole, and the setting is validated |
| `internal/ingress` `TestGatewayRouteAttachmentNeedsAllowedRoutes`, `TestGatewayAllowedRoutesAll`, `TestGatewaySelectorNotSupported`, `TestGatewayCertificateRefStaysInNamespace` | Third audit round, multi-tenancy: an HTTPRoute in another namespace does not attach without `allowedRoutes`, `from: All` admits it, a `Selector` is refused with a warning rather than silently admitted, and a `certificateRef` naming another namespace is refused |
| `internal/tlsconf` `TestCertificateTransparency` (extended) | Third audit round, Certificate Transparency: two timestamps from one log satisfy a requirement of one log, not two; a second independent log does satisfy it; presence-only counting (no log list) counts distinct logs too |
| `internal/filters/accountguard` `TestDefaultBlocksAreNotAccountKeyed`, `TestDefaultStepsStillCountTheSource`; `internal/challenge` `TestClientFaultExcludesTheProxysOwnLimits`; `internal/kinds/http` `TestThirdPartyInducedRequests` | Third audit round, the defences turned on their users: no built-in class has a block rung keyed on the account or on account_ips while every class still blocks on the address, the pair or the device; a full verification table and a spent nonce do not count against the client while a wrong answer does; a prefetch, a cross-site and a same-site fetch are induced while a scanner sending no fetch metadata, a typed URL and a same-origin fetch are not |
| `internal/secret` `TestWorldReadableKeyringIsRefused`, `TestKeyringLineTooLongIsAnError` | Third audit round, keys: modes 0644, 0604 and 0660 are refused with the reason, 0600 and 0640 load; a keyring line too long for the scanner is an error instead of silently dropping the keys after it |
| `internal/admin` `TestUsersFileIsRereadWhenItChanges`, `TestUnreadableUsersFileKeepsTheCurrentSet`, `TestEmptiedUsersFileRevokesEveryone`, `TestSessionEndsWhenTheUserIsRemoved`, `TestSessionLosesOperatorRightsWhenTheRoleIsLowered` | Third audit round, revocation: a user removed from the file stops resolving and one added starts, a lowered role applies, a malformed file keeps the previous set and reports the failure, an emptied file revokes everyone; a live session of a removed user is refused with 401 and a demoted operator can still read but no longer act |
| `internal/ldap` `TestBind`, `TestSearch`, `TestParseFilterErrors`, `TestEscape` | The LDAP client against an in-process fake server: a simple bind (valid, wrong and empty password), a search with an equality filter and returned attributes, an AND filter and a non-match; the RFC 4515 filter parser's accepted and rejected forms; filter and DN escaping, and that an escaped injection payload cannot break out of a filter |
| `internal/filters/ldapauth` `TestDirectBind`, `TestSearchBindWithGroup`, `TestValidate` | The ldap_auth filter end to end: a direct bind accepting a valid login and refusing a wrong or empty password and a DN-injection username; a service-account search then bind enforcing group membership, stripping `Authorization`, forwarding the user, and refusing a non-member; the configuration validation cases |
| `internal/upstream` `TestDiscoveryHTTPList`, `TestDiscoveryHTTPConsul`, `TestDiscoveryHTTPFailureKeepsSet` | HTTP registry discovery: the `list` format with an auth header, a filled-in default port, a per-entry weight and canary flag, a bad address dropped and a duplicate collapsed; the `consul` format taking only passing instances, the node-address fallback and the passing weight; a failed poll (500) keeping the previous endpoint set and counting the error |
| `TestRoutingByRegexAndHeaders` | Header exact match, cookie presence, an anchored path pattern with `strip_prefix`, a failing pattern falling back to the prefix route, cleaned paths |
| `TestProxyProtocolInbound`, `TestProxyProtocolUntrustedPeer` | An `http` listener with `proxy_protocol` behind a trusted peer: v2 and v1 headers set the client address seen by the upstream and by `deny_cidrs`, `LOCAL` keeps the balancer, a trusted peer without a header is dropped without reaching the upstream and counted; from an untrusted peer the header is not parsed and a plain request works |

### Fuzz targets

| Target | Input | Property |
|--------|-------|----------|
| `config.FuzzParse` | arbitrary bytes | never panics; nil config implies error |
| `router.FuzzMatch` | host, path, method | never panics on any strings |
| `netutil.FuzzCleanPath` | path | output always starts with `/` |
| `netutil.FuzzHost` | host header | never panics |
| `netutil.FuzzReadProxyHeader` | bytes | never panics; a parsed header is version 1 or 2 and, unless LOCAL, carries two addresses of one family |
| `logging.FuzzParseTemplate` | access log template | `ParseTemplate` and `ValidTemplate` agree; a non empty valid template yields tokens |
| `dns.FuzzParseMessage` | wire message | never panics (compression loops included); record counts equal the header counts; the question end lies inside the message; names at most 253 bytes |
| `dns.FuzzParseTrustAnchor` | DS or DNSKEY line | never panics; a parsed anchor names a zone |
| `config.FuzzUnifiedDiff` | two texts | never panics; changed exactly when the line sequences differ; every hunk line comes from an input |
| `capture.FuzzWriteFlow` | request bytes, response bytes, a comment and an endpoint pairing | never panics; the pcapng that comes out walks block by block to the end with every length agreeing at both ends of each block, a captured length inside its block and an original length never below it |

Fuzz corpora that find failures are committed under `testdata/fuzz`.

### Scale and load

`make scale` runs `TestScale` at the 1.0 target size and prints the
measurements; `make bench` runs the routing, limiter and metrics
benchmarks; `make load` starts the load backend and a proxy on loopback
and drives vegeta at `RATE` for `DURATION`. `test/load/README.md` has the
procedure and the rules for a comparable number; `docs/PERFORMANCE.md`
records the results.

### Packaging and policy (CI `package` job)

Runs in a Fedora container: `make selinux` compiles the module against the
Fedora policy headers (which catches interface names that only exist in
upstream reference policy), `make rpm` builds the three packages offline
from the vendored tarball, `make rpmlint` checks them against
`deploy/rpm/xproxy.rpmlintrc`, the packages are installed with `dnf` and
the three binaries print their version. What the container cannot do is
load the module or run with SELinux enforcing; that is the Fedora VM item
under planned additions and the release checklist.

### Static and supply chain

`golangci-lint` (errcheck, gosec, staticcheck, govet, bodyclose, noctx,
errorlint, gocritic and more), `go vet`, `gofmt`, `govulncheck`. All run in
CI (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`) on every push and pull request.

## Coverage

`make cover` instruments every package under `internal/` for every test
binary (`-coverpkg=./internal/...`), so an integration test in
`internal/kinds/http` counts towards the packages it exercises, and runs under
the race detector. `make cover-gate` then applies two rules through
`test/covergate`: the core packages together must reach 80 %, and no
single package may fall below 60 %. Both numbers are Makefile variables.
Excluded from the gate: every `main` package, the test fakes
(`acmetest`, `icaptest`, `filtertest`, `testutil`), `version` and the
`filters` registration list, and `sandbox`, whose Landlock, seccomp and
capability code runs in a confined child process that the parent's
profile cannot observe (the child's probes assert the effects instead).
CI fails on either rule (ASR-Q2).

`internal/sandbox` is also left out of the coverage run itself, and only
that run: its tests apply Landlock and seccomp to a child process, which
then cannot write the coverage file the instrumented binary emits on
exit, so the run fails on a package whose figure the gate ignores
anyway. `make test` and `make test-race` run it in full.

A `main` package is excluded by where it lives rather than by name:
every one in this repository is under a `cmd` directory, the released
programs under the top level `cmd/` and the `go:generate` helpers under
a `cmd/` beside the package they generate for. Each is a flag parse and
an error print over a package that is gated on its own, and a list
naming them one by one is a list that goes stale silently — the gate
stops failing to mention the newest generator.

`-coverpkg=./internal/...` so a package's figure includes what other
packages' tests reach in it):

and the refusals are tested; inheriting a real descriptor needs
systemd), the QUIC transport internals beyond the handshake, relay and
admission tests, the error paths that need a failing `crypto/rand`, a
failing `fsync` or a write that fails after the file was created, and
file system failures other than a full disk and the permission and path
cases in `internal/secret/filesystem_test.go`.

## Mutation testing

`make mutate` runs [gremlins](https://github.com/go-gremlins/gremlins) on
the packages whose comparisons and arithmetic decide admission:
`internal/limits` (token buckets, peer rates, connection and concurrency
limits), `internal/router` (host and path precedence) and
`internal/netutil` (client address and host normalisation). Gremlins
applies one mutation at a time (flip a comparison, move a boundary, change
an operator, invert a sign) and runs the package tests; a mutant that
survives marks a behaviour no test observes. `.gremlins.yaml` sets the
gate at 75 % efficacy (killed over killed plus lived) and 90 % mutant
coverage. Timed out mutants are excluded from efficacy; run it on an
otherwise idle machine, the timeouts derive from a baseline run.

| Package | Mutants | Killed | Lived | Efficacy |
|---------|---------|--------|-------|----------|
| `internal/limits` | 68 | 59 | 9 | 86.8 % |
| `internal/router` | 31 | 25 | 6 | 80.6 % |
| `internal/netutil` | 46 | 45 | 1 | 97.8 % |

The first run scored 61.8 %, 79.3 % and 87.0 %; the survivors led to
`limits/mutation_test.go` (exact token, eviction, peer staleness, peer
count and shard bound boundaries, the hash function, per address
connection counts), `TestTieBreaks` and `TestWildcardHostEdges` in the
router, and `TestHostEdges` in netutil. What still lives are boundary
mutants on the sort comparator for equal length prefixes (equivalent
under stable sort with the index tie break) and on comparisons whose
boundary case is unreachable by construction (an IPv6 literal without a
closing bracket at position zero, `len(host)+1`). They are accepted and
listed here so a future change to those lines is looked at.

The kinds gremlins does not cover get the same treatment by hand: every
check a listener makes is neutered one at a time, the package's tests are
run, and a mutation nothing notices is either a missing test or code that
was not doing anything. The NTP and NTS gateway's sweep of fifty-five
mutations is what added the tests for the association bound, the egress
list, a server's unreadable answer, the monitor's hysteresis and its
unsynchronised state, the learning run's two enforcement points and the
elapsed-clock assertions; it also sharpened the era test, which claimed to
cross an era without doing so, and the wrong-length digest assertion, which
accepted any refusal rather than the one that names the mistake. Two
mutations survive and are accepted: replacing the forwarded bytes with a
freshly rendered packet, in either direction. They survive because `Parse`
and `Bytes` are exact inverses for every packet the parser accepts, which
is itself asserted (`TestEveryAcceptedPacketRendersBackToItself`) -- and
the relay still forwards the octets that arrived, so that guarantee does
not depend on the renderer staying lossless.

## Chaos tests

`internal/kinds/http/chaos_test.go` injects the failures an operator sees in
production and asserts continuity, bounded recovery and no leak: a reload
storm, a flapping endpoint, an upstream dying mid response, a full log
disk and certificate rotation with a broken file. The table above lists
what each one checks. Two of them changed the product: a full disk now
counts dropped events (`log_write_errors`, `xproxy_log_write_errors_total`)
and warns on stderr at most once a minute instead of failing silently,
and the earliest certificate expiry per listener is exposed as
`xproxy_certificate_expiry_seconds` so a forgotten renewal is an alert,
not an outage.

## Manual smoke procedure

Used before every merge to `main` until binary level tests exist:

```sh
make build
python3 -m http.server 18080 --bind 127.0.0.1 &
cat > /tmp/x.yaml <<'EOF2'
version: 1
server:
  listeners: [{name: main, address: "127.0.0.1:18081"}]
management: {socket: /tmp/xproxy-mgmt.sock, socket_mode: "0600"}
logging: {directory: /tmp/xproxy-logs}
rate_limits: [{name: rl, rate: 5, burst: 3}]
upstreams: [{name: py, endpoints: [{address: 127.0.0.1:18080}], health_check: {path: /, interval: 1s, timeout: 500ms}}]
routes: [{name: all, upstream: py, rate_limits: [rl]}]
EOF2
mkdir -p /tmp/xproxy-logs
./bin/xproxy -config /tmp/x.yaml &
curl -i http://127.0.0.1:18081/                          # 200, X-Request-Id, no Server header
for i in 1 2 3 4 5; do curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code} " http://127.0.0.1:18081/; done   # 200 200 429 429 429
./bin/xproxyctl -socket /tmp/xproxy-mgmt.sock status
./bin/xproxyctl -socket /tmp/xproxy-mgmt.sock upstreams
./bin/xproxyctl -socket /tmp/xproxy-mgmt.sock -config /tmp/x.yaml reload
tail -1 /tmp/xproxy-logs/security.log; cat /tmp/xproxy-logs/audit.log
kill %2; kill %1
```

### Transparent interception against a real firewall rule

The socket options and the loop check are unit tested, and a listener
that would dial itself is driven end to end -- that is the dangerous case
and it needs no privilege. What no test can produce is a connection whose
original destination is somewhere else, because that needs a firewall
rule and `CAP_NET_ADMIN`. On a Linux host, as root:

```console
# ip rule add fwmark 1 lookup 100
# ip route add local default dev lo table 100
# iptables -t mangle -A PREROUTING -p tcp --dport 8080 -j TPROXY       --on-port 9000 --tproxy-mark 1
```

with a listener on 9000:

```yaml
- name: intercept
  address: "0.0.0.0:9000"
  kind: tcp
  tcp:
    original_destination: true
    allow_destinations: ["10.0.0.0/8"]
    destination_ports: [8080]
```

Then from a client, `curl http://10.0.0.5:8080/`. What to check:

- the access log line names `endpoint 10.0.0.5:8080`, not the listener's
  address: the destination came from the socket;
- a request to a destination outside `10.0.0.0/8` is refused with
  `destination_not_allowed` and nothing is dialled;
- with `transparent: true` as well, `tcpdump` on the upstream shows the
  **client's** source address. Without the `ip rule`/`ip route` pair
  above, that connection hangs and the log says `upstream_unavailable` --
  which is the failure the load-time warning is about.

### Pseudo terminal check of the TUI

Before release the TUI is driven in a pseudo terminal against a running
proxy (a Python `pty.fork` of `xproxyctl tui`, sending `2`, `3`, `5`,
`6`, `0`, `1`, `q`), asserting that the alternate screen is entered and left,
that each screen shows live data (upstream address, a ban, sparklines,
security events) and that the process exits with status 0.

### Browser check of the GUI

Run the smoke set-up above, create users and start the GUI:

```sh
echo 'operator-password-1' | ./bin/xproxy-admin user add op -role operator -users /tmp/admin-users
./bin/xproxy-admin serve -listen 127.0.0.1:18203 -socket /tmp/xproxy-smoke/mgmt.sock -config /tmp/xproxy-smoke/xproxy.yaml -users /tmp/admin-users
```

Then drive headless Chromium over the DevTools protocol (a Node script
with the built-in `WebSocket`, no packages): navigate to the page, submit
the login form, switch `location.hash` through every screen, enrol a user on the MFA
page and check the secret, the QR code and the recovery codes appear
once and survive until dismissed, then remove that enrolment again, run
*Validate* on a broken edit and *Validate and save* on a good one, unban,
switch the log view to `access` and request a page through the proxy. The
expected result is every screen rendered with data, the problems list
naming the bad key, the saved file with `.bak` next to it, the new access
line arriving through the event stream, and no Content Security Policy
violation or script error in the browser log (the only console errors are
the deliberate 401 before login and the 502 for a feature the smoke
configuration does not enable). This procedure was run on the reference
build; it becomes an automated job once the Fedora runner exists.

### Browser check of the challenge

The challenge script is verified in a real browser before release, using
the headless Chromium that CI images carry:

```sh
# xproxy running with challenge: {difficulty: 14} and a route with
# challenge: {mode: always}, upstream serving "hello from backend"
curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" http://127.0.0.1:18121/        # 503: the page
chromium --headless --no-sandbox --virtual-time-budget=60000 --dump-dom http://127.0.0.1:18121/
# prints the backend document; the access log shows 503, 200 (script),
# 303 (verification) and 200 (return), and challenges_passed is 1
```

## Planned additions

Phase 2:

- WAF corpus tests: the CRS regression suite run through the proxy in
  detect mode, asserting scores per rule family; a false positive suite from
  sample applications. (The engine level tests above cover the integration;
  the corpus run is still open.)
- Cluster convergence bounds under load and a three node partition test
  (the two node functional tests exist; see above).
- HTTP/3 interoperability with `quic-go` clients and a curl build.
- Load tests on the reference hardware (8 cores, remote generator); the
  `test/load` suite, the container baseline and the soak script exist,
  the 24 hour soak and the 8 core numbers are still to be run.
- Fuzz targets for ICAP framing, WAF transaction building, redaction rules
  and the cluster wire format.
- ACME against Pebble in a container in CI (the fake CA in
  `acmetest` covers the protocol; Pebble adds its deliberate failures and
  nonce rejection).

Phase 3:

- Binary level end-to-end suite in `test/e2e` that runs `bin/xproxy` with
  socket activation emulated via `LISTEN_FDS`, exercises `xproxyctl` and
  checks logs.
- Chaos: certificate expiry while serving (the certificate itself expiring,
  as opposed to rotation, which is covered), a SIGKILLed upstream process
  (the mid-response death is covered at the connection level).
- Fedora VM runner: install the RPMs, enable units, run traffic, assert no
  AVC denials and a passing `systemd-analyze security` band (the container
  job covers build, lint and install).
- Automated browser job for the GUI (the DevTools procedure above) on the
  same runner.
